#345. Google rolls out Gemini 4 Hydrogen, finally replacing 3.8 Flash

Gemini 4 Hydrogen isn’t getting a slow, security-conscious release like Carbon and Argon.

Google is continuing its elemental AI naming scheme with the new Gemini 4 Hydrogen. These names are less descriptive than the Pro/Flash monikers of earlier releases, but Hydrogen’s purpose is clear: It replaces the faster, more economical Flash models most people in Google’s ecosystem use.

With Google’s recent Argon and Carbon releases, the rollout was staged and began with cybersecurity researchers. Even when general availability began, Google restricted access to users paying for its most expensive AI subscription. The limits for Hydrogen are much more relaxed, with access beginning immediately. This model has replaced Gemini 3.8 Flash across the company’s products.

Google claims that Hydrogen represents a major improvement versus the previous Flash model. It runs in one of four thinking levels (low, medium, high, and the new xhigh), and even in high mode, it’s 2.5 times faster than Flash 3.8. Plus, it reduces “fabricated claims” by more than 5x, according to Google’s analysis. Users will also see gains in code generation, with Hydrogen more than tripling 3.8 Flash’s score in Terminal-Bench. In some cases, Hydrogen even trades blows with Argon. It managed a score of 54 in the popular Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index with xhigh mode versus 50 for Argon.

Google believes Hydrogen will be a key part of how developers work with agentic coding tools. While it still positions Argon and Carbon as the best for long-horizon tasks and security, Hydrogen’s speed makes it ideal for generating simpler code and running tests without blowing through token budgets.

Hydrogen is also being integrated with Google Search, which the company says is the biggest update to its AI-powered search tools ever. The new model should better handle long, multipart questions, and the responses in both AI Overviews and AI Mode will have more visual elements. The company also claims Hydrogen’s fan-out search has been tuned to link to more sources, but we’ve heard that from Google before.

#343. Pre-orders for some Apple Home Collection devices will open November 13

You can lock in orders for two LG cameras and a video doorbell.

On the same day that the Apple HomeView smart display and refreshed Apple TV 4K hit stores, pre-orders will open for some accessories in the Apple Home Collection. Apple confirmed to Engadget that you'll be able to pre-order the LG indoor camera, outdoor camera and video doorbell on November 13.

Those three devices, which Apple and LG collaborated on, will be the first Apple Home Collection accessories to ship. They're slated to arrive in December. Apple hasn't revealed exactly when other products in the lineup — including thermostats and door locks — will be available, but the company has said some will be available next month with the rest debuting in early 2027.

The indoor camera can capture 2.5K footage of what's going on in your home while you're away, giving you a way to keep an eye on your pets, for instance. It has low-light color night vision and infrared (IR) night vision support. The camera, which costs $200 and can detect people and pets, can tilt up when you're out and down when you're home — this can be managed automatically through the Home app or manually by pressing a button.

The outdoor camera can recognize faces, pets and deliveries. It too supports low-light color night vision, IR night vision and 2.5K video capture. The camera has IP65-rated protection from rain and dust and a two‑way talk function. As with the indoor camera, you'll get 10 days' worth of event video history via iCloud+. There's built-in storage too. The standard outdoor camera costs $300 and a version with a floodlight will run you $400.

As for the $250 video doorbell, that has a dual-camera design that can pick up (for instance) visitors and packages simultaneously — a 2.5K camera faces forward and a Full HD one points downward. You'll be able to view either feed separately in the Home app and chat to whoever's at your door using the two-way talk function. When someone rings the doorbell, HomeView and HomePod devices will sound a chime. You'll get notifications on other Apple devices too.

Again, there's low-light color night vision and IR night vision support, along with event video history in iCloud+ and built-in storage. The camera can detect people, pets, vehicles and packages and it has a dimmable spotlight.

#342. The Elder Scrolls' most obscure adventure gets a 4K PC makeover

N-Gage exclusive Shadowkey gets an AI-coded glow-up with plenty of new features.

So, you think you’re an Elder Scrolls superfan? You say you’ve been there since The Elder Scrolls Arena on MS-DOS and have logged hundreds of hours in The Elder Scrolls Online? You say you’ve bought all of the roughly 5,000 re-releases and remasters of Skyrim that have been made available in the last 15 years?

Tell me, then: What do you think of The Elder Scrolls Travels: Shadowkey?

What’s that? You’ve never played Shadowkey? You weren’t there day one back in 2004, Nokia N-Gage in hand, ready to experience the game’s 176 x 208 resolution mobile hack-and-slash RPG combat for yourself? And you call yourself a real Elder Scrolls fan?

Well, don’t worry. Now you can fill in this crucial gap in your history without even having to track down functional, decades-old N-Gage hardware. That’s thanks to Shadowkey: Reforged, a free fan-made PC port that pairs updated art with the original game files and scripts for an upscaled, 4K experience that runs natively on PC, no N-Gage or emulator needed.

Moving from a pre-iPhone mobile game to a modern PC obviously comes with major graphical upgrades, powered by a completely rewritten PC graphics engine. What was once a cramped portrait orientation on the N-Gage has now been expanded for a 16:9 monitor standard that displays at up to 120 fps (though the game’s logic still matches the original game’s 25 fps). In addition to newly retextured character and environmental models, the new port also sports small touches like bloom lighting, rippling water, and an upgraded interface with sharper text rendering than was possible on an ancient mobile phone.

Using the original game files (which you can obtain from an easy-to-find legitimate copy of the game, of course) means that Reforged‘s quests, dialogue, and game rules are “worked out from the original program,” as the game’s website puts it. That said, the game’s “Modern” mode adds a few touches like smarter enemy AI, fall damage, searchable containers, and a fast travel system that are missing from the original game (and from Reforged‘s more authentic “Classic” mode). The Modern mode also includes some welcome quality-of-life improvements like controller rumble, a smoother on-screen interface, and “better death poses.”

Those with a disposition against vibe-coded clones and ports should know that Reforged author Dan Whelan writes that he used “AI to assist with coding and building the engine, and with debugging,” as well as for the creation of “most of the modern, higher resolution artwork.” But those who are OK with this AI assistance should be happy to see the project moved from its first playable version in late October to a full-featured “release day” build for Windows, Mac, and Android that came out Wednesday. Whelan has been releasing daily updates to the project and seems set to continue, if the current “Version 0.2.9” designation is any indication.

The inherent limitations of 2000s-era N-Gage game development (which helped earn the original release some middling review scores) are still pretty apparent in even this Reforged remake. Still, it’s nice that Elder Scrolls completionists can now experience one of the series’ least-played releases. At the very least, it’s something to tinker with as we all continue the long wait for Elder Scrolls VI.

#341. Apple Sports adds live UFC updates

The app can deliver real-time stats for each fight on a UFC card.

UFC fans now have another way to keep up with the action. The Apple Sports app will provide live updates from the MMA organization's events, starting with this Saturday's UFC 334 card. That event will feature two major title fights. Ciryl Gane will defend the Heavyweight Championship against Josh Hokit and Amanda Nunes will challenge Kayla Harrison for the Women's Bantamweight Championship.

If you're not able to tune into UFC 334 (or future events) on Paramount+, you can keep up to date with the action via "enhanced data visualizations and dynamic on-screen animations" on Apple Sports. You'll be able to see the tale of the tape and fighter rankings before each bout, including prelims. Once the fights are underway, you can get real-time updates, including on stats like total strikes, significant strikes, takedowns, knockdowns, submission attempts and control time. Live Activities will provide real-time updates on Apple Watch and the iPhone lock screen too.

The Sports app debuted in February 2024 with support for basketball, hockey and soccer. Other sports have joined the mix since — including football and tennis — and Apple brought it to many more markets ahead of the men's World Cup earlier this year.

#340. This Defense Makes Drones (and GPS) Think They’re in Peru

GPS-linked devices have been insisting that Ukrainians are in Lima, the Peruvian capital. The culprit is a jamming system built to thwart Russian airstrikes.

Reporting from somewhere in Ukraine. Or Peru.

The S.U.V. in front of us suddenly pulled over. The driver had been trying to lead us to antennas near Kyiv, the Ukrainian capital, that scramble the signals of Russian drones and missiles, but his GPS had gone haywire.

“Apologies. Some technical issues,” texted Serhii, a spokesman for Cascade Systems, the Ukrainian defense company that made the system. He asked that only his first name be used, out of concern for his safety.

We should have known: The S.U.V.’s navigation system had decided that the vehicle was in Lima, Peru, 7,628 miles southwest of Kyiv.

This happens all the time to Ukrainians. When Cascade’s antennas are switched on to misdirect Russian bombardments, the GPS in phones and cars is often misdirected, too — to that particular South American city.

This was now happening to a few of the very guys behind the mapping chaos. They had been hoisted on their own antennas, so to speak.

The system, called Lima for obvious reasons, blasts out electromagnetic interference meant to strip the eyes and ears from Moscow’s missiles and drones, making them think they are somewhere else.

#339. Shania Twain calls for equal pay and more diversity in country music

Shania Twain is standing up for others in country music.

The legendary star in the genre used her acceptance speech for the Equal Play Award at Sunday’s CMT Awards to call for more equity and diversity in the country music industry.

“When I wrote the phrase ‘Man I feel like a woman’ I honestly had no idea at the time it would become an undercurrent of a decades-long career,” she said, referring to one of her biggest hits. “It’s amazing how one statement can empower so many.”

The phrase, Twain said, “has been adopted by an array of fantastic communities all around the world and become a genuine path of power…which brings me so much joy.”

“I believe in an all-inclusive country music. We’re family,” Twain said. “This is a genre of music with a rich history that raised and nurtured my own songwriting and performance and recording career from childhood. Currently, the industry standard does a real disservice to this.”

Twain, who was presented the award by rapper Megan Thee Stallion, shouted out some of the diverse acts she is spotlighting on her “Queen of Me” tour this year, including Lily Rose, Lindsay Ell, Hailey Whitters, Mickey Guyton and others.

“I will continue to do my best as a trailblazer,” Twain said. “Together, let’s ensure all our fellow artists get equal play regardless of gender, age or race.”

She also performed acappella with the African American singing group, The BoykinZ, while presenting an award onstage. Twain connected with the group, who are sisters and rising country stars, after Kelly Clarkson surprised them recently by bringing Twain out to meet them during an episode of Clarkson’s daytime talk show.

#337. Zelenskyy says Starshield has been slowed again, endangering Ukraine's defense

It comes three weeks after Ukraine’s speeds were restored following the last throttling incident.

As media headlines focus on Jensen Huang's resignation and the midterms fallout, you could almost forget that Ukraine is still fighting for its life — with all of Europe nervously watching. According to President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, his job just got harder again. The country's access to Starshield, the military version of Starlink, has been hit with "systematic speed restrictions" for several days. And it just so happens to be while Trump's envoys are in Moscow for talks with Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Zelenskyy posted on X that the speed limits are being imposed along the war's entire front line and in Russian-occupied territory. He said any terminal moving faster than 161 km/h (100 mph) loses its connection to the satellite network. Following briefings from his defense minister and top commander, Zelenskyy said Ukraine's military had investigated and ruled out random errors.

"Unfortunately, this is helping the Russians kill more Ukrainians and carry out more accurate strikes against Ukraine," Zelenskyy said. "We are trying to establish all the circumstances with the American side."

Ukraine's interceptor drones rely on Starshield to chase down Russia's jet-powered Shahed drones, which can fly up to 500 km/h (310 mph). Trying to catch them at 100 mph won't cut it. Zelenskyy says some strikes have already gotten through because Ukraine's interceptors couldn't go fast enough.

The timing is... curious. US envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner landed in Moscow today for talks with Putin. They're expected to meet Zelenskyy later at a summit in Ireland.

This isn't the first time Ukraine's Starshield access has been throttled. On October 14, Ukraine's military found that its speeds were being limited to around the same threshold. It happened as President Trump was publicly complaining about Ukraine's strikes on Russian refineries, which he blamed for high fuel prices in the US ahead of the midterms. Analysts agree that Trump's war with Iran has been the biggest factor in sky-high oil and diesel prices.

It still isn't clear who made the call to throttle Starshield the first time. Reuters, citing US officials, reported that the order came from inside the White House. But days later, the Wall Street Journal reported that Musk personally ordered the slowdown, possibly after talking with Trump. Trump denied any role, for whatever that's worth.

Musk's responses were all over the place. First, he said Starshield is "owned and controlled by the US government," then later claimed Ukraine had violated Starlink's terms of service. That's a strange thing to say about something you just said you don't own or control. Regardless, speeds were restored on October 24, reportedly after Trump called Musk and asked him to lift the caps amid bipartisan pressure from lawmakers.

Three weeks later, we're back where we started, with (so far) no one claiming responsibility.

#336. Ars Live: What drone attacks on data centers mean for modern life in the cloud

A livestream Ars discussion is happening on November 17 at 1 pm Eastern Time.

Drone and missile strikes have inflicted unprecedented damage on data centers in Eastern Europe and the Middle East this year, disrupting cloud services for millions of people and permanently deleting some Amazon Web Services customer data. This trend represents a new concern for Big Tech companies—but also for governments and everyone else who depends on common digital services in modern life.

To explore what this all means for modern society’s reliance on cloud resilience, I will be hosting a live conversation titled “Drone attacks and data centers: New Risks for Big Tech” on Tuesday, November 17 at 1 pm ET with Joseph Jarnecki, a senior research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute (RUSI), a UK defense and security think tank. He leads RUSI’s work on how cloud services factor into national security and defense, along with tech sovereignty efforts.

Jarnecki has done extensive research on how Ukraine strengthened its cloud resilience with Western partners despite facing both cyberattacks and kinetic attacks at the start of the full-scale Russian invasion, and explored what that means for NATO and European governments’ cloud adoption plans.

This year, Jarnecki has written about Iran’s drone and missile strikes on Amazon data centers in the Middle East during the US-Israeli war with Iran, and the broader implications for governments and Big Tech. He has also commented on the impacts of Russia and Ukraine exchanging strikes on each other’s data centers.

For more background on the data center attacks that have transpired so far in 2026, you can see my previous Ars coverage of how the Iranian strikes left Amazon with months of repairs, and caused enough damage to result in the permanent loss of some AWS customer data.

I’ve also covered why Russia was not ready for the data center war it started with Ukraine, with the systematic Russian strike campaign targeting Ukraine’s data centers provoking Ukrainian counterstrikes that damaged Russian government data centers and AI data centers owned by the Russian tech giant Yandex.

My conversation with Jarnecki will explore why data centers have become much more prominent military targets, whether defending or hardening data centers against drones and missiles is a realistic option, how cloud resiliency can be improved in wartime conditions, and the choices governments and cloud providers face in deploying data centers during an era of growing geopolitical risks.

You can watch the discussion live on YouTube on November 17 at 1 pm ET. We will also be fielding a few audience questions.

#335. Ex-NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang is set to testify before Congress this month

House Foreign Affairs Chair Brian Mast said the hearing will take place before Thanksgiving.

Jensen Huang might have stepped down from his roles at the top of NVIDIA, but he won't be sliding out of the spotlight just yet. The company's former CEO and president is set to testify before Congress by Thanksgiving, according to House representatives.

House Foreign Affairs Chairman Brian Mast said Huang agreed to testify on Wednesday — hours before NVIDIA announced his resignation with immediate effect — after lawmakers threatened him with a subpoena. It appears the hearing will take place behind closed doors (which is unfortunate, because that's likely to be some spicy testimony). However, Mast expects it to be filmed and transcribed, according to Bloomberg. The House's Intelligence and China-focused committees will join the Foreign Affairs cohort for the hearing.

"Jensen and I have been very at odds as some of you already know on export controls, but NVIDIA is a very important company to the United States of America," Mast said. "I don't want to see anything hurt in NVIDIA, but he has some very serious questions to answer."

NVIDIA has been in turmoil after it emerged over the weekend that the company was investigating Huang over an affair to which he admitted, allegedly with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy. President Donald Trump said the FBI is investigating the situation as well.

On Wednesday, it was reported that Huang had another affair, this time with Kimberly Powell, an NVIDIA executive who reported to him. The company's board met later that day to determine Huang's future. Colette Kress, NVIDIA's executive vice president and chief financial officer, is stepping in as interim CEO while the company seeks a permanent successor.

Meanwhile, the Department of Justice is reportedly investigating Alice Huang, the alleged spy, to determine whether entities associated with her were involved in attempts to smuggle Super Micro Computer products to China. The DoJ is also said to be taking the lead in an investigation into her former company, the Singapore-based Megaspeed International, over alleged smuggling of US-made chips into China in defiance of export restrictions. The company said it would cooperate with the probe.

Officials in Singapore opened an investigation into Alice Huang, Megaspeed and associated individuals last year. The Ministry of Home Affairs said in a statement on Thursday that she was barred from re-entering the country after police determined she knowingly offered up inaccurate information to downplay her role in Megaspeed. However, they "concluded that there was insufficient evidence to establish that Huang had committed any offences relating to the possible inaccurate declarations for which Megaspeed was being investigated." The investigation into Megaspeed and six other individuals is ongoing.

#334. A Museum’s ‘Wanted’ Poster Campaign Brings Looted Painting Back to France

The Orléans Fine Arts Museum is on a quest to recover hundreds of works stolen during World War II — and the director wants your help.

Olivia Voisin discusses her “cold cases” like a police detective, but she is actually chief of the Orléans Fine Arts Museum in France, searching for paintings missing since the Nazis occupied the city in 1940.

The museum launched a campaign this summer to track down nearly 400 works looted during World War II. It created “Wanted” posters with images of lost art, plastering them in and around the city of Orléans and flooding the internet. Now, the first case has been solved.

An amateur sleuth in France discovered one of the looted paintings last month in Switzerland, as it was set for sale. Now instead of being put up for auction, “The Drinker,” a 17th-century work by the Dutch artist Willem Cornelisz Duyster, will soon be returned to Orléans.

It will eventually be displayed at the museum in a gallery dedicated to the missing works — with the hope that some day it will be joined by other recovered paintings, Ms. Voisin said.

The museum’s campaign to reclaim its missing collection comes as France has taken steps to make it easier to repatriate artwork looted in war or during colonization.

“The auction house was very cordial,” Ms. Voisin said of the Swiss business that had advertised the Dutch work. It had labeled the painting as a preparatory study, unaware that it was the known to be missing final version.

#333. In reversal, American Cancer Society urges routine prostate cancer screening

Men aged 50–69 and at average risk should get PSA screening every two years.

In a significant update to its guidelines for prostate cancer screening, the American Cancer Society is now recommending that men get routinely screened for prostate cancer with a PSA (prostate-specific antigen) test.

Men at average risk should get a PSA test every two years from age 50 to 69, according to the new guidelines released Thursday.

Men considered at high risk due to race and family history should start testing earlier, at 45, and most are recommended to get tested every year. Specifically, yearly testing starting at age 45 is recommended for all Black men, who have higher rates of prostate cancer. Non-Black men should also get yearly testing starting at 45 if a close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer before age 65, or if more than one of their relatives has had prostate cancer. On the other hand, if only one close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer and it was diagnosed at age 65 or older, non-Black men in this case should start PSA screening at 45, but are recommended to get testing only every two years.

Men ages 70 to 74 should consult their doctor on continuing screening, while those 75 and up are not recommended to get screened.

The new guidance departs from ACS’s previous iteration, published in 2010, which made no universal recommendations for routine screening. Men at age 50 were recommended to use shared-decision making with their doctors on whether or not to get tested. The recommendation was similar to that from the US Preventive Services Task Force, a federal panel that regularly evaluates evidence and makes recommendations on such preventive medical care. (The USPSTF is currently working on an update to its prostate cancer screening recommendation, though it should be noted that the panel has recently been overhauled by anti-vaccine Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and new members are reportedly rife with conflicts of interest.)

In announcing the guidelines today, the ACS says its updates are based on new evidence since 2010 that shows screening saves lives, and that advances in case management can avoid the concerning harms from overdiagnosis and overtreatment. For many men with prostate cancer, their cancer will never grow or cause harm in their lifetime, while treatment can run the risk of erectile dysfunction and urinary incontinence.

In the new guidance, the ACS adds guardrails, firstly advising all men to be informed about the risks and benefits. If a PSA test comes back high—defined as 3 ng/mL or higher for men under 70 and 6.5 ng/mL or higher for those 70 and up—the ACS recommends repeating the test before taking any next steps.

If repeat testing is still high, then men should get either a prostate MRI or a validated biomarker test to assess risk. Only if there is a high suspicion of cancer—such as having a very high PSA test result—should men jump to a prostate biopsy.

For men diagnosed with prostate cancer, active surveillance should be the initial management strategy for men whose cancer appears to be at low risk of growing or spreading.

“By catching aggressive cancers with a simple PSA blood test, you open the door to highly effective treatments that protect your future,” Dr. Shanthi Sivendran, interim chief patient officer for the ACS, said in a statement. “Meanwhile, lower-risk findings can be safely managed through careful, active surveillance.”

Prostate cancer is one of the most common cancers in men and the second leading cause of cancer death in US men, the ACS notes. Nearly 334,00 men are estimated to be diagnosed with prostate cancer in 2026, and it’s estimated that over 36,320 will die from the disease.

This post was updated for clarity on November 12.

#332. 2023 March Madness: Will UConn or San Diego State triumph in the national championship game that 'means everything?'

The University of Connecticut won its fifth men’s basketball national title with a 76-59 victory over San Diego State University on Monday night at NRG Stadium in Houston.

Senior guard Tristen Newton led UConn (31-8) with 19 points and 10 rebounds while Final Four Most Outstanding Player Adama Sanogo, a junior forward, chipped in with 17 points and 10 rebounds.

“We weren’t ranked going into the year so we had the chip on our shoulder,” UConn head coach Dan Hurley told game broadcaster CBS. “We knew the level that we could play at, even through those dark times,” he added, referencing the team’s six losses in eight games during the regular season.

He said going into the tournament his group had confidence garnered during the season.

“And when you have the type of leaders like Andre Jackson (game-high six assists Monday) and Adama Sanogo, they kept this team together, got us back on track and we knew we were the best team in the tournament going in and we just had to play to our level,” he added.

San Diego State (32-7) was topped by Keshad Johnson who had 14 points.

UConn trailed very early but San Diego State was undone by an 11-minute, eight-second stretch in which they scored just five free throws and missed 12 consecutive shots from the field. The Huskies went from down 10-6 to up 36-24 at halftime.

The Aztecs made a run midway through the second half and narrowed the deficit to five at 60-55 with 5:19 to play but the Huskies scored the next nine to take a comfortable lead into the final two minutes.

“We battled. Battled back to five in the second half, but gave them too much separation,” San Diego State coach Brian Dutcher said. “We had to be at our best. We weren’t at our best. A lot had to do with UConn.”

Senior guard Adam Seiko told reporters they gave themselves a chance with their second half comeback but UConn “just made a little bit more plays” at the end.

“They have a lot of weapons. They were pretty good,” said Matt Bradley, also a senior guard. “To beat them, we had to make shots. I shot poorly. And you had to have a really good game to beat those dudes on the offensive end.”

UConn won each of its six tournament games by at least 10 points, with its closest game being a 13-point win over the University of Miami in the national semifinals.

“I just want to thank my teammates, my coaches who believed in me. If it were not for them I would not be here right now,” Sanogo told CBS.

Jordan Hawkins, who scored 16 points for UConn, talked about winning the crown one day after his cousin, Angel Reese of Louisiana State University, won the women’s title.

“I mean it’s absolutely amazing that we both get this opportunity and I mean the family reunion is going to be great so that’s all I know,” he said.

UConn enters rarefied air as only the sixth team to win five NCAA men’s basketball championships, joining UCLA (11), Kentucky (eight), North Carolina (six), Duke (five) and Indiana (five). All of UConn’s titles have come since 1999 with the most recent before Monday occurring in 2014.

UConn’s women’s teams have won 11 basketball national titles.

CNN’s Steve Almasy and Issy Ronald contributed to this report.

#331. Multiple Microsoft 365 services are experiencing an outage

Outlook and Teams are among the platforms impacted by “elevated load” issues.

Microsoft is experiencing an outage for several of its services this afternoon. Initial reports focused on users being unable to access their email in Outlook, but more than the one platform in its Microsoft 365 suite appears to be down. The company's support account on X acknowledged the issue.

At 2:15PM ET, many Engadget staff were unable to even load Microsoft's service status page. Those who were saw a message that listed Exchange Online, Microsoft Teams, SharePoint Online, Microsoft Copilot 365 and Microsoft Defender XDR as the potentially impacted services. While the status report points to Business and Enterprise tiers of the 365 tools, individual users also appear to be impacted. The full update states:

"We're investigating an issue impacting multiple Microsoft 365 services and we've determined the affected infrastructure is experiencing elevated load across multiple regions, which is likely contributing to the connectivity errors. We're restarting and repairing impacted portions of this infrastructure to restore capacity, and reviewing an unexpected increase in a specific type of service traffic to determine whether it's contributing to the elevated load. In parallel, we're evaluating options to redirect traffic to alternate infrastructure to expedite relief."

Around 5PM ET, Microsoft Planner, Microsoft To Do, Microsoft Intune, Microsoft Defender for Office 365 and Microsoft Purview had been added to the list of impacted services. Issues with Power BI and Microsoft 365 Apps Admin Center were also noted. The latest update reveals that a fix for this afternoon's disruptions still may not be imminent.

"We're continuing to pursue all available pathways to restore service, as our actions taken thus far have not provided the anticipated relief," the status page states. "Our investigation has found that a core process on the affected servers is becoming overwhelmed, which causes the servers to stop responding and contributes to the capacity loss."

Update, Nov 13, 9:09am ET: "We're seeing continued recovery progress across affected regions, with broader improvements in North America, sustained improvements across much of Europe, and encouraging recovery signals in Asia Pacific," Microsoft wrote on X. "Some users may still experience intermittent access issues or delayed email delivery as mitigation continues across remaining affected infrastructure. Our focus remains on stabilizing recovered infrastructure, completing mitigation in remaining impacted areas, and reducing delayed email queues and backlogs while we validate service consistency across connection methods."

#329. Who is Colette Kress, NVIDIA's interim CEO?

Kress spent 25 years in various finance roles before joining NVIDIA in 2013

After 33 years as the chief executive officer of NVIDIA, Jensen Huang has resigned from the AI and gaming giant he founded. On Thursday morning, the company's board of director announced Colette Kress, NVIDIA's current executive vice president and chief financial officer, as Huang's temporary replacement.

"The Board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team, and we expect the company's operations, customer commitments and product roadmap to continue without interruption," newly-minted NVIDIA board chair Harvey Jones said in a release announcing Thursday's news.

Kress is a relative unknown, but is taking lead of NVIDIA at tumultuous time in the company's history. Huang departure comes days after NVIDIA's board said it would investigate Huang after he admitted to having an affair with a former tech executive accused of being a Chinese intelligence asset. The New York Times later reported that Huang had also had an affair with an NVIDIA executive who reported to him. Before the revelations, the company did not have a succession plan in place. And for at least the immediate future, it's now on Kress to lead the company. Here's what we know about the executive.

Kress worked at three companies before joining NVIDIA. She first spent eight years in finance roles at Texas Instruments before joining Microsoft in 1997. In 2006, six years after Steve Ballmer took over as Microsoft's CEO, she became the chief financial officer of the company's Server and Tools division. She remained with the company until 2010, when she moved to Cisco. There, she was the senior vice president and chief financial officer of the company's Business Technology and Operations Finance unit.

To hear Kress tell it, Huang went out of his way to recruit her. Sometime before she joined NVIDIA in 2013, the CEO apparently sketched his vision for the company on a whiteboard. Per a 2023 interview, Huang wanted to transform NVIDIA into the dominant player in the nascent AI industry. Say what you will about Huang's personal life and judgement, the man was an AI visionary.

It would be almost another four years following Kress joining NVIDIA before a group of Google researchers published "Attention Is All You Need," the paper that introduced the world to transformer models, and nearly a decade before the release of ChatGPT. Kress partly credits her sons, who were only eight and 10 at the time, for buying Huang's pitch. Despite their young age, the two already knew of NVIDIA and its importance to gaming. In a 2024 Fortune interview, Kress said NVIDIA was the smallest company she had worked for when she joined in 2013.

At the start of her tenure, NVIDIA's revenue was $4.28 billion for the year. In fiscal 2026, the company recorded a record $215.9 billion in revenue.

When NVIDIA's board of directors announced Kress as Huang's temporary successor, it said it would consider both internal and external candidates to take on the role of CEO permanently, leaving the door open for her and other current NVIDIA employees to win the job. In any case, it's far too early to speculate who the company's board might interview for the job. But if you can forgive a writer a tired analogy, if Kress stays on as CEO, she is likely to be a Tim Cook-like figure, one that can stabilize the company and guide it toward continued growth.

"The Board, [executive staff] and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication," Kress wrote in her first email to NVIDIA employees after the transition was announced, according to CNBC.

Still, she has a very difficult job ahead of her. Huang has agreed to testify before Congress in a hearing that the House aims to hold before the Thanksgiving holiday. The chair of the House Foreign Affairs committee, Brian Mast of Florida, said he wasn't ruling out the possibility of "public hearings for other people or other things down road." You can bet NVIDIA is likely to face an intense amount of scrutiny in the coming weeks, particularly when Democrats take control of the House and Senate at the start of next year.

#328. Lots of Competition to Catholicism in Peru as Pope Returns for Visit

Pope Leo spent nearly two decades in Peru, rising to bishop and becoming a citizen. But many Peruvians have turned to evangelical churches, seeking a more expressive and direct connection to God.

Pope Leo spent nearly two decades in Peru, rising to bishop and becoming a citizen. But many Peruvians have turned to evangelical churches, seeking a more expressive and direct connection to God.

Members of Agua Viva, a Pentecostal megachurch in Lima, Peru, at a prayer concert on Sunday.Credit...

Strobe lights scanned a crowd of outstretched arms as a rock band took the stage, smoke billowing out and applause echoing through the coliseum. The pastor bounced out to the beat of a drum roll, a microphone curved around his face.

This Sunday’s sermon, Pastor Sergio Hornung announced, would be about “the territory still to conquer.”

“There are many places where they talk to you about God,” said Scarlet Reyes, 21, a former Catholic who started coming to this Pentecostal church, Agua Viva, two years ago. “But here, they teach you how to know God.”

Pope Leo arrives on Wednesday in Peru, where he served as a priest for two decades, rose to bishop and became a Peruvian citizen. He returns at a time when the church faces two major challenges in Latin America: growing secularization and competition from other Christian denominations.

While Catholicism remains the dominant religious force in the country and the region, evangelical churches have made enormous gains, with congregants saying these churches offer more direct relationships with God and a more expressive, modern style of worship.

#327. Specialized’s Tarmac SL9 S-Level is built to wreck your Zone 2 ride

Specialized’s second-fastest bike may be the smart buy.

I love a good Zone 2 ride. There’s nothing like getting on the bike, settling into a groove, and letting the miles (and hours) flow by. But sometimes when I hopped on the Specialized Tarmac SL9 S-Level, Zone 2 would turn into a tempo ride. It was like having a little imp—the stereotype dressed in red with the pitchfork—on my left shoulder whispering into my ear: “Go fast. This bike was built to go fast. Speed is good. You can do a Zone 2 ride tomorrow.” Normally, I’d count on the good imp—the dude with the harp and wings—on my right shoulder to restrain these urges. But the Tarmac is so seductive that he would just shrug and say “he’s got a point.” And in a moment of weakness, I would drop the hammer and head into the red, just because the SL9 makes that so rewarding.

The Tarmac SL9 is Specialized’s aero road bike, with the S-Level sitting just below the S-Works in Specialized’s top tier of carbon, aero road bikes, where it replaced the Tarmac SL8 Pro. After it got an update this year, I spent several weeks riding it, putting nearly 1,000 miles on it

Specialized offers the Tarmac SL9 in four models, all available with SRAM and Shimano drivetrains (Ars tested the SRAM version). Notably, you don’t need to buy the top-of-the-line bike to get the top-of-the-line groupset. Here’s the breakdown of the Tarmac SL9 lineup (note that the Shimano versions of the Expert is 20 g lighter and Comp is 70 grams heavier than the SRAM versions):

The $10,500 S-Level offers the top-of-the-line SRAM Red groupset and integrated cockpit, just like the $14,000 S-Works. But the frameset, wheelset, and saddle differ. The major difference is the frame: the S-Level gets the same carbon-fiber FACT 10r frame as the Expert and Comp. At 822 g for a 56 cm frame, the FACT 10r is 135 g heavier than the FACT 12r used on the S-Works. Despite the weight difference, Specialized says the FACT 10r offers the same stiffness and comfort characteristics as its lighter sibling. Along with the frame, different wheelsets and saddles make the S-Level 500 g heavier than the S-Works. With bottle cages, a Garmin mount, and my preferred Shimano XTR XC PD-M9200 MTB pedals, the S-Level I tested weighed in at 7.98 kg. By way of comparison, my own road bike—a Gen 8 Trek Madone—weighs in at 7.71 kg.

I rode the S-Level all around the Chicago suburbs as well as in central Illinois. Unlike the Diverge 4 Pro, I wasn’t able to test its climbing ability in the Rocky Mountains. All we’ve got in most of Illinois are short, punchy climbs … and a lot of long flats. I rode The S-Level on everything from freshly laid asphalt to freshly laid chipseal, including a couple of 60-mile-plus rides on the country roads downstate.

Visually, the S-Level is striking. The blue-on-white with gray striping is one of Specialized’s better-looking colorways. And it looks aerodynamic. Indeed, Specialized says the Tarmac SL9 is the most aerodynamic road bike it has ever made. Notable changes from the previous-generation Tarmac SL8 to SL9 involved tweaking the head tube, rerouting the rear hydraulic brake hose to accommodate the narrower head tube, and moving to a dropped-down tube for improved aerodynamics. But the most visually striking change is the Win Fin. As the seat tube rises from the bottom bracket, it deviates from the vertical to follow the curve of the wheel as it approaches the seat stays. When riding with just a single water bottle on the downtube—common in pro races where a full bottle is just a team car away—Specialized claims Win Fin saves riders half a watt.

The S-Level I tested came with Specialized’s 30 mm Specialized Cotton TLR tires—used by Red Bull-BORA-hansgrohe on the UCI World Tour—in a tubeless setup. According to Bicycle Rolling Resistance, the Cotton TLR outperforms the much-loved Continental GP 5000 S TR tires when it comes to rolling resistance (at 28 mm). Using Silca’s tire pressure calculator, I rode with the tires inflated to 73 psi in the front and 75 psi in the rear, a departure from the 62/64 setting on my 32 mm Contis—and there is a slight, but noticeable difference in ride comfort between the S-Level and my own road bike. Mounted on the Roval Rapide CL III carbon wheels with 21 mm internal width, the Cotton TLR tires measured 30.2 mm wide (my 32 mm Contis are a hair over 33 mm).

On every surface shy of brand-new chipseal, the Tarmac felt comfortable and balanced, with the bike absorbing some of the punishment meted out by uneven pavement. Fresh chipseal is another matter entirely, as it feels more like gravel, which felt jarring on the SL9 S-Level. What was particularly impressive about the SL9 is how great it feels on long rides. Some manufacturers are skilled at building aero bikes that feel great on hours-long rides, and the Tarmac is just as good as the Trek Madone SLR 9 AXS Gen 8 I reviewed in this regard.

When you’re ready to drop the hammer, the S-Level is your willing accomplice. It’s twitchy in all of the good ways, with instantaneous response to the rider’s effort. The SRAM Red AXS groupset makes shifting buttery smooth (and the bonus buttons on the brake hoods allow the rider to change screens on the bike computer without releasing the brake hoods), and the drivetrain (50t/37t chainrings and a 10t-36t cassette) offers gear ratios for every type of terrain.

The first few seconds of a climb on the Tarmac SL9 S-Level feel like they come for free. Whether I stayed seated or cranked out watts in an upright position, there was no lag or flex as the bike snapped forward. Descents were amazing, and cornering on the S-Level is sublime—I felt balanced and in control at all times.

There are a few nits to pick. The reach to the brakes in the drops felt excessive on my first couple of rides, and I had to adjust them slightly to brake easily on descents. Also, I would prefer shorter cranks on the Tarmac. The trend among professional riders has been toward shorter cranks because it makes holding an aero position easier and reduces hip and knee flexion. To its credit, Specialized reduced the crank length from 172.5 mm on the 56 cm SL8 to 170 mm on the same-sized SL9, but I run 165 mm cranks on my road bikes and noticed the difference on the S-Level. One demographic that buys expensive road bikes is middle-aged dudes with empty nests and disposable incomes, and they are the ones most likely to appreciate shorter cranks.

The 3D-printed Power Pro Mirror is a fine saddle, but there’s a reason I ride with a Specialized S-Works Power with Mirror on my Trek—it’s more comfortable and offers noticeably more padding around the cutout. And if I owned the S-Level, I’d move to 32 mm tires as soon as I wore out the 30s for better compliance.

Specialized brags that the Tarmac SL9 S-Works is the fastest bike ever made, and it claims the S-Level is the second-fastest bike ever made. I can’t speak to that claim, but the SL9 S-Level checks all of the boxes for an aerodynamic road bike. It’s lightweight, it’s fast, it’s responsive, and it’s great on rollers and punchy climbs. And it’s nice to get a SRAM Red groupset without springing for the absolute top-of-the-line model. $10,500 is a hefty sum for a road bike, but I’d be a lot more willing to forego the weight savings from the lighter components (frame, wheels, seatpost, bottom bracket, and bearings) of the S-Works—and spend $3,500 less. The real selling point, though, is how often it made me want to go harder just for the fun of it. That little imp has a point: Zone 2 can wait until tomorrow.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#324. Meta's Muse can now make calls on your behalf (if you have a paid subscription)

The AI agent can schedule appointments, make reservations and wait on hold for you.

Meta's Muse just got another ability that could make the AI agent feel a little bit more like an actual personal assistant. Muse is now able to place phone calls on your behalf, if you have a paid subscription.

With the change, Muse is able to call businesses to help with tasks like making reservations, scheduling appointments and other "calls you don't want to make," Meta's Chief AI officer Alexandr Wang said in an update. The feature is available to those who have paid for a separate Muse subscription, which comes with additional tokens and some extra features.

Some users who have had access to the feature already have reported that Muse has been able to handle phone-related tasks with relative ease. Moltbook creator and Meta Superintelligence employee Matt Schlicht shared a recreation of a phone conversation his agent had with a Volvo dealership last month, noting that it was able to wait on hold in order to get the info he needed.

My muse called Volvo for me to see when the next available appointment is.

I have it do multiple calls like this every day (I'm still trying to get better and better at leveraging muse).

My muse called, waited on the line for 15 minutes,... pic.twitter.com/bosH1tyW1R

— Matt Schlicht (@MattPRD) October 9, 2026

Interestingly, in the call shared by Schlicht, Muse doesn't explicitly identify itself as an AI agent though it notes it's calling his behalf and that it's transcribing the call. That may be enough for some straightforward tasks, but there's always a chance these types of calls could go off the rails or the person on the other end hangs up when they realize they're speaking with an AI.

Meta has been steadily churning out new features for Muse, which has already drawn more than 10 million users, according to the company. People can also now assign a dedicated email address to their agent in order to loop it into important emails without giving wholesale access to their inbox.

The company isn't the first to experiment with phone-calling abilities for AI. Google has introduced a number of similar features over the years, including "Call For Me" for Pixel phones and Instinct recently introduced a "Concierge" feature for its agent that's meant to handle more complex calls.

#323. Nvidia Scandal Unveils a Fierce Spy War Raging Behind Trump and Xi’s Diplomacy

That the United States suspected Nvidia’s leader was a Chinese spy target underscores the deep distrust between the two powers, even as their leaders try to stabilize ties.

As President Trump and China’s leader, Xi Jinping, prepare to meet next week to extend a fragile trade truce, a fresh espionage scandal has shown that behind their diplomacy is a fierce intelligence rivalry.

At its center is Jensen Huang, the Nvidia leader and an influential ally of Mr. Trump’s, who has acknowledged having an affair with a Chinese businesswoman whom U.S. intelligence officials suspect of spying for Beijing. China has denied the espionage allegation.

Under Mr. Xi, the Chinese Communist Party has built a more sophisticated and assertive spy service that regularly highlights the importance of technological security and of threats posed by the United States. At the same time, the Central Intelligence Agency has said that Chinese companies are a legitimate target for espionage in areas like artificial intelligence, semiconductors and biotechnology.

Nvidia’s computer chips power the global A.I. boom and sit at the center of Washington’s efforts to keep that technology out of China’s hands. That makes Mr. Huang, who has lobbied Mr. Trump to ease restrictions on the export of advanced chips to China, a prime target, analysts say.

“It is not at all surprising, given the centrality of Nvidia to U.S.-China tech competition, and the importance of tech competition in the overall U.S.-China rivalry, that someone like him would be a top target for Chinese intelligence collection,” said Sheena Chestnut Greitens, an expert on Chinese security at the University of Texas at Austin.

#322. BYD admits wheel assembly error on its flagship electric SUV

Some Great Tang SUVs had their wheels installed the wrong way.

BYD has apologized for an assembly mistake affecting some units of its newly released Great Tang — or Datang, as it's called in China — electric SUV. According to Bloomberg, several owners complained that the wider wheel rims that were supposed to be installed on the rear of the vehicle were installed on the front. Meanwhile, the narrower wheel rims for the front of the car were installed on the back instead.

The Chinese electric automaker has posted its apology on social network Weibo, explaining that it launched an investigation after getting complaints from owners. Apparently, it traced the problem to its Hubei-based wheel mesh assembly supplier. BYD said it has already fixed the issue, and the supplier has also added a visual inspection mechanism on the production line, so that future vehicles will no longer be affected.

BYD didn't say how many vehicles were affected by the assembly error, but it said that there was no safety risk associated with the wheels being installed the wrong way. Even so, it will replace the wheel rims of the those vehicles for free and will also offer their owners a replacement vehicle or compensation for what they will spend on transportation while their Great Tang SUVs are being repaired. In addition, the company will restart their vehicle's warranty period.

The company unveiled the Great Tang SUV in March, with prices starting at around $40,000. It received 150,000 orders in its home country. BYD is planning to release the electric vehicle in European and Asian markets outside China by the end of this year or in early 2027.

#321. Rocket Report: Starship set for historic test flight; a century of Electron

“You can simplify it as a big tank feeding engines.”

Welcome to Edition 9.19 of the Rocket Report! Will something historic happen this weekend? No orbital rocket has ever been fully recovered with the intent of reusing all of its components. But that’s just what SpaceX is going to try to do with a Starship launch on Friday, and an upper stage landing attempt 22.5 hours later on Saturday afternoon, local time. Ars will be on hand in Starbase, Texas, to provide coverage of it all.

Electron set for milestone launch. The next launch of Rocket Lab’s Electron vehicle is due to lift off from the Mahia Peninsula in New Zealand on November 14 at 5:30 UTC. This mission will serve as Electron’s 100th overall mission—a notable milestone for the small rocket that first flew in 2017, NASASpaceflight.com reports.

A true commercial success … This flight of Electron, named “The Mountain God Rises,” will deliver another iQPS satellite into LEO for iQPS’s synthetic aperture radar constellation. The anniversary rocket features a special gold-and-black livery which looks fantastic. Rocket Lab truly does deserve recognition as the world’s second purely commercial launch business to make a successful go at it. We are all celebrating with them.

Is the Vega rocket a dead end? Last month, Italian rocket builder Avio presented a market update that outlined the additional investment required to increase the annual launch cadence of its Vega C rocket to six and eventually nine flights per year, European Spaceflight reports. The need for this additional funding raises an interesting question about the future of Italy’s Vega launch vehicle family. Is investing in the infrastructure needed to secure just three more Vega C flights per year by the end of the decade the best use of resources, particularly when several emerging European launch companies appear poised to offer comparable capacity?

Boosters may be better used by Ariane 6 … Vega C isn’t going anywhere. But it’s an inescapable reality that a single Ariane 64 launch requires the equivalent of an entire year’s worth of solid rocket boosters for Vega C at its baseline annual launch cadence of four flights. Does it make sense to continue investing in expanding Vega C’s launch cadence and later introducing Vega E when the future of both vehicles depends on production capacity that is increasingly demanded by Ariane 6?

Synspective inks 10 Alpha rockets. Firefly Aerospace announced on Tuesday a multi-launch agreement with Synspective, a Japanese synthetic aperture radar satellite data and solutions provider, for up to 10 launches on Firefly’s Alpha rocket through 2030. With the ability to launch 1,000 kg to low Earth orbit, Alpha will deliver two of Synspective’s SAR satellites to orbit per mission, deploying up to 20 total satellites.

Getting it done with one ton … Under the agreement, Firefly will launch Synspective’s SAR satellites from both Pad-0A at Wallops Flight Facility in Virginia and Space Launch Complex 2 at Vandenberg Space Force Base in California. “This landmark agreement reflects growing customer confidence in our Alpha Block II vehicle,” said Jason Kim, CEO of Firefly Aerospace. “With 1-ton payload capacity and responsive launch capabilities, Alpha is a natural fit for customers like Synspective seeking direct access to orbit to get their operations up and running sooner.”

Stoke and Planet sign multi-launch agreement. Stoke Space on Monday announced a multi-launch agreement with Planet Labs PBC. The companies did not announce the terms of the agreement, including the total number of launches under contract, nor the cost of the launches. “We need launch partners who move as quickly as we do,” said Will Marshall, Co-Founder & CEO of Planet.

An open-ended agreement? … The announcement suggests this could be an open-ended agreement, however. “Planet builds and operates the largest fleet of Earth-imaging satellites in orbit and captures imagery of Earth’s landmass every day,” a related release says. “Operators like Planet launch on a regular basis to expand and replenish their fleets, and that steady demand is what our Nova fleet is designed to serve.”

The launch crunch comes to national security. The US military is taking a new approach to prop up commercial launch companies as SpaceX looks to wind down its Falcon rocket program, suggesting defense officials see the workhorse rocket’s retirement as a potential national security risk, Ars reports. The Pentagon’s Office of Strategic Capital has twice in the last two weeks announced it was sending cash to two of SpaceX’s competitors in the launch business.

More pads and rockets … First, on October 26, the office announced a $150 million conditional loan commitment to Stoke Space. Military officials revealed a second $150 million Pentagon loan Tuesday for Rocket Lab. Stoke Space’s loan is tailored to support the buildout of the company’s second launch pad at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida. The loan to Rocket Lab will support the company’s efforts to increase the flight rate of its partially reusable Neutron rocket.

Rocket Lab raises Neutron price. Citing strong customer demand and a constrained market for launch, Rocket Lab announced this week it had increased the cost of a dedicated Neutron rocket from $55 million to $70 million, Payload reports. In recent months, the coming shortage of launch capacity in the US—mainly due to SpaceX sunsetting its Transporter rideshare program—has sparked a flurry of interest in new medium-lift rocket entrants.

Positive on Neutron … “Neutron is rapidly establishing itself as the backbone of next-generation constellation deployment and government missions in the medium-lift launch class,” CEO Peter Beck said in a statement. “Strong international demand and a growing launch manifest, at a new price point, is proof that Neutron is the launch vehicle the market has been waiting for.” The price hike for Neutron isn’t keeping customers away as new bookings continue, the company said. Customers’ next options to fly on Neutron are now in the 2030s and beyond.

Rockets are not hard? During an hour-long event on Thursday, Abel Avellan, the founder and chief executive of the telecommunications company AST SpaceMobile, laid out his vision for developing a large rocket to launch his Bluebird satellites into orbit, Ars reports. Avellan emphasized, over and over again, that AST plans to build a “simple” rocket. He paraphrased SpaceX founder Elon Musk several times, saying, “the best part is no part.”

This sounds a little naive … “Of course rocket engineers are going to get mad with me, but you can simplify it as a big tank feeding engines,” he said of the rocket. “So big tanks—we know how to do them. We know how to do them. We know how to do them in composite.” The Blacklift rocket will be “simple,” he said, because it will be solely tailored to lift five Bluebird satellites into low-Earth orbit at a time. It will have 20 metric tons of lift capacity, a reusable first stage, and launch twice a month from a pad at Wallops Flight Facility in Virginia that formerly served the Antares rocket. (submitted by thegooglemonster)

When will the Eclipse rocket be ready? During an earnings call this week (see transcript), Firefly CEO Kim spoke about the company’s ongoing work to develop the Eclipse rocket. “We’re heads down on Eclipse,” he said. “We’re laser-focused on delivering the first stage to our co-developer Northrop Grumman. And we’re over 175 hot fires on Miranda, counting the one from today. And we’re looking at all the data, verifying the capability. It’s a very mature engine, and we’re very confident in its capabilities.”

Probably not launching soon … However, when an analyst pressed on Firefly getting a launch in 2027, Kim did not offer a direct answer. “Everything is just laser-focused on all the milestones in terms of qualifying Miranda and qualifying our composite tanks and fluid systems as well,” he said. “So we’re really, really just excited about the future of Eclipse, especially with this injection of capital that will help accelerate our production line.” Bottom line: Don’t expect to see Eclipse on a launch pad any time soon.

Starship set for 15th test flight on Friday. Flight 15 of Starship is preparing to launch as soon as Friday, November 13, pending regulatory approval, SpaceX said. The 75-minute launch window will open at 4:45 p.m. CT (22:45 UTC). The upcoming flight is planned to be the first to return both Starship and Super Heavy to the launch site for catch. If successful, this will mark the first full recovery of an orbital launch system.

All eyes on the ship … Starship’s initial return-to-launch-site mission is expected to orbit the Earth at an altitude of about 275 km and complete approximately 14 orbits around the planet over the course of a more than 22-hour flight. Starship’s primary mission will be to deliver 26 Starlink V3 satellites to orbit. But really, this mission is all about the landings, first of the booster and then, more dramatically, of the upper stage Starship on Saturday.

SpaceX is now an ‘infrastructure’ company. For a long time, SpaceX has been synonymous with developing newer, larger, and ever-more-reusable rockets, Ars reports. But that began to change over the last decade with the introduction of Starlink, and the change has accelerated during the last 18 months as the company has pivoted to data and artificial intelligence. At last week’s UP.Summit conference in Florida, SpaceX’s vice president of launch, Kiko Dontchev, offered some of the most revealing comments to date about the company’s changing focus.

From AI to Mars? … “In the last couple years, when I talk about SpaceX, yes, we are a rocket company, but fundamentally we are an infrastructure company at this point,” Dontchev said. “We’re building infrastructure on the ground and low Earth orbit, soon on the Moon, and obviously eventually Mars.” However, Dontchev insisted that human settlement and Mars remain part of the core of SpaceX’s aspirations. “We have come to understand that this tool is critical to us accelerating our pace of innovation,” he said of artificial intelligence. “Without AI, I think we still would have gotten to the Moon. I think we still get to Mars with SI. We’re going to get there so much faster.”

Nov. 13: Vulcan | Amazon Leo LV-01 | Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida | 17:54 UTC

Nov. 13: Starship | Flight 15 | Starbase, Texas | 22:45 UTC

Nov. 14: Soyuz 2.1b | MKA Kinosputnik, others | Vostochny Cosmodrome, Russia | 00:50 UTC

#320. The haunting Masters meltdown that changed Rory McIlroy's career

Editor’s Note:  This story was originally published in April 2023.

Slumped on his club, head buried in his arm, Rory McIlroy looked on the verge of tears.

The then-21-year-old had just watched his ball sink into the waters of Rae’s Creek at Augusta National and with it, his dream of winning The Masters, a dream that had looked so tantalizingly close mere hours earlier.

As a four-time major winner and one of the most decorated names in the sport’s history, few players would turn down the chance to swap places with McIlroy heading into Augusta this week.

Yet on Sunday afternoon of April 10, 2011, not a golfer in the world would have wished to be in the Northern Irishman’s shoes.

A fresh-faced, mop-headed McIlroy had touched down in Georgia for the first major of the season with a reputation as the leading light of the next generation of stars.

An excellent 2010 had marked his best season since turning pro three years earlier, highlighted by a first PGA Tour win at the Quail Hollow Championship and a crucial contribution to Team Europe’s triumph at the Ryder Cup.

Yet despite a pair of impressive top-three finishes at the Open and PGA Championship respectively, a disappointing missed cut at The Masters – his first at a major – served as ominous foreshadowing.

McIlroy shot 74 and 77 to fall four strokes short of the cut line at seven-over par, a performance that concerned him enough to take a brief sabbatical from competition.

But one year on in 2011, any lingering Masters demons looked to have been exorcised as McIlroy flew round the Augusta fairways.

Having opened with a bogey-free seven-under 65 – the first time he had ever shot in the 60s at the major – McIlroy pulled ahead from Spanish first round co-leader Alvaro Quirós with a second round 69.

It sent him into the weekend holding a two-shot cushion over Australia’s Jason Day, with Tiger Woods a further stroke behind and back in the hunt for a 15th major after a surging second round 66.

And yet the 21-year-old leader looked perfectly at ease with having a target on his back. Even after a tentative start to the third round, McIlroy rallied with three birdies across the closing six holes to stretch his lead to four strokes heading into Sunday.

The youngster was out on his own ahead of a bunched chasing pack comprising Day, Ángel Cabrera, K.J. Choi and Charl Schwartzel. After 54 holes, McIlroy had shot just three bogeys.

“It’s a great position to be in … I’m finally feeling comfortable on this golf course,” McIlroy told reporters.

“I’m not getting ahead of myself, I know how leads can dwindle away very quickly. I have to go out there, not take anything for granted and go out and play as hard as I’ve played the last three days. If I can do that, hopefully things will go my way.

“We’ll see what happens tomorrow because four shots on this golf course isn’t that much.”

The truth can hurt, and McIlroy was about to prove his assessment of Augusta to be true in the most excruciating way imaginable.

His fourth bogey of the week arrived immediately. Having admitted to expecting some nerves at the first tee, McIlroy sparked a booming opening drive down the fairway, only to miss his putt from five feet.

Three consecutive pars steadied the ship, but Schwartzel had the wind in his sails. A blistering birdie, par, eagle start had seen him draw level at the summit after his third hole.

A subsequent bogey from the South African slowed his charge, as McIlroy clung onto a one-shot lead at the turn from Schwartzel, Cabrera, Choi, and a rampaging Woods, who shot five birdies and an eagle across the front nine to send Augusta into a frenzy.

Despite his dwindling advantage and the raucous Tiger-mania din ahead of him, McIlroy had responded well to another bogey at the 5th hole, draining a brilliant 20-foot putt at the 7th to restore his lead.

The fist pump that followed marked the high-water point of McIlroy’s round, as a sliding start accelerated into full-blown free-fall at the par-four 10th hole.

His tee shot went careening into a tree, ricocheting to settle between the white cabins that separate the main course from the adjacent par-three course. It offered viewers a glimpse at a part of Augusta rarely seen on broadcast, followed by pictures of McIlroy anxiously peering out from behind a tree to track his follow-up shot.

Though his initial escape was successful, yet another collision with a tree and a two-putt on the green saw a stunned McIlroy eventually tap in for a triple bogey. Having led the field one hole and seven shots earlier, he arrived at the 11th tee in seventh.

By the time his tee drive at the 13th plopped into the creek, all thoughts of who might be the recipient of the green jacket had long-since switched away from the anguished youngster. It had taken him seven putts to navigate the previous two greens, as a bogey and a double bogey dropped him to five-under – the score he had held after just 11 holes of the tournament.

Mercifully, the last five holes passed without major incident. A missed putt for birdie from five feet at the final hole summed up McIlroy’s day, though he was given a rousing reception as he left the green.

Mere minutes earlier, the same crowd had erupted as Schwartzel sunk his fourth consecutive birdie to seal his first major title. After starting the day four shots adrift of McIlroy, the South African finished 10 shots ahead of him, and two ahead of second-placed Australian duo Jason Day and Adam Scott.

McIlroy’s eight-over 80 marked the highest score of the round. Having headlined the leaderboard for most of the week, he finished tied-15th.

Tears would flow during a phone call with his parents the following morning, but at his press conference, McIlroy was upbeat.

“I’m very disappointed at the minute, and I’m sure I will be for the next few days, but I’ll get over it,” he said.

“I was leading this golf tournament with nine holes to go, and I just unraveled … It’s a Sunday at a major, what it can do.

“This is my first experience at it, and hopefully the next time I’m in this position I’ll be able to handle it a little better. I didn’t handle it particularly well today obviously, but it was a character-building day … I’ll come out stronger for it.”

Once again, McIlroy would be proven right.

Just eight weeks later in June, McIlroy rampaged to an eight-shot victory at the US Open. Records tumbled in his wake at Congressional, as he shot a tournament record 16-under 268 to become the youngest major winner since Tiger Woods at The Masters in 1997.

The historic victory kickstarted a golden era for McIlroy. After coasting to another eight-shot win at the PGA Championship in 2012, McIlroy became only the third golfer since 1934 to win three majors by the age of 25 with triumph at the 2014 Open Championship.

Before the year was out, he would add his fourth major title with another PGA Championship win.

And much of it was owed to that fateful afternoon at Augusta. In an interview with the BBC in 2015, McIlroy dubbed it “the most important day” of his career.

“If I had not had the whole unravelling, if I had just made a couple of bogeys coming down the stretch and lost by one, I would not have learned as much.

“Luckily, it did not take me long to get into a position like that again when I was leading a major and I was able to get over the line quite comfortably. It was a huge learning curve for me and I needed it, and thankfully I have been able to move on to bigger and better things.

“Looking back on what happened in 2011, it doesn’t seem as bad when you have four majors on your mantelpiece.”

McIlroy’s contentment came with a caveat: it would be “unthinkable” if he did not win The Masters in his career.

Yet as he prepares for his 15th appearance at Augusta National this week, a green jacket remains an elusive missing item from his wardrobe.

Despite seven top-10 finishes in his past 10 Masters outings, the trophy remains the only thing separating McIlroy from joining the ranks of golf immortals to have completed golf’s career grand slam of all four majors in the modern era: Gene Sarazen, Ben Hogan, Gary Player, Jack Nicklaus, and Tiger Woods.

A runner-up finish to Scottie Scheffler last year marked McIlroy’s best finish at Augusta, yet arguably 2011 remains the closest he has ever been to victory. A slow start in 2022 meant McIlroy had begun Sunday’s deciding round 10 shots adrift of the American, who teed off for his final hole with a five-shot lead despite McIlroy’s brilliant 64 finish.

At 33 years old, time is still on his side. Though 2022 extended his major drought to eight years, it featured arguably his best golf since that golden season in 2014.

And as McIlroy knows better than most, things can change quickly at Augusta National.

#318. Whoop Humans is a social network designed for fitness enthusiasts

Members can only share data from their Whoop wearables as guarantee of authenticity.

Whoop, the company behind the popular screenless wearable trackers, has launched a social network for users who like sharing their exercise details and fitness efforts called Whoop Humans. It's a rather unusual name, so you might be wondering how the company decided on it. Well, Will Ahmed, the CEO of Whoop, described it as the "most verifiably human network ever launched." Members can share their fitness data on the social network, including their sleep, recovery, strain and activities information. In all cases, the data comes from a Whoop wearable. That's "real-world information that cannot simply be generated as a conventional text post" at a time when "traditional social networks are increasingly filled with artificial content," the company said in its announcement.

Whoop already has Teams, which connects users through groups and leaderboards. But the new social network lets users follow friends and family directly to comment and react to their posts. The company envisions a social network, wherein people can check in on each other based on their progress. The idea is for members to be able to congratulate each other when they reach their goals or to be able to remind a friend to take it easy if they see the other's "Recovery" data is low. Hard to imagine most people taking an interest in another's fitness information, but people who are very much into fitness might see value in the social network.

In addition to being able to share fitness data, members will also be able to share photos from their workouts with a feature called "Moments." They can keep those Moments private, however, and, thankfully, they can choose which workout types or achievements appear on the social network. Just because they're wearing a Whoop doesn't mean their data will automatically be shared. Members can still update their sharing preferences any time they want. Whoop Humans is now live and available through the company's app.

#317. Workers in Spain Stage a General Strike for the First Time in 14 Years

Workers in a wide range of industries walked off the job to protest rising rents and stagnant wages, which have become central issues in the campaign.

Spanish workers staged a general strike on Wednesday for the first time in 14 years, protesting the rising rents and stagnant wages that have become central issues in the national election to be held this month.

Unions said millions of workers participated in the walkout, while hundreds of thousands of Spaniards joined rallies in Madrid, Barcelona, Valencia and other cities in the evening.

Early on Wednesday, protesters blocked roads in Madrid, set up picket lines outside major factories in Catalonia and disrupted transportation across Spain.

Police officers wielding shields and batons forced hundreds of demonstrators away from the Gran Vía, the central thoroughfare in Madrid, which rally goers had blocked. The anger of protesters in the capital was directed largely at “rentistas” — a Spanish term for people who live off rental income.

Later in the day, during a march through central Madrid that drew 120,000 protesters according to the government, demonstrators chanted in a festive mood, “One jump, two jumps, whoever doesn’t jump is a rentista.”

Clashes with the police erupted in Barcelona, where a group of masked protesters broke away from the crowd after the demonstration ended, according to local news media. The group smashed several shop windows, threw pyrotechnic devices at police officers and tried unsuccessfully to storm an Apple Store, the reports said.
