#290. Disney's 3D movies shine on Meta's Quest 3, it's just a shame it took so long

Finally, you can watch Avatar in 3D HFR at home.

3D movies have been, and continue to be, a bit of a gimmick. But the technology can also be used well — just take a look at James Cameron's Avatar films, or CG movies like Moana and Zootopia. With the death of 3D TVs, though, there haven't been many easy ways for consumers to see 3D content at home. Enter the VR headset: For years, intrepid VR owners have been ripping 3D Blu-rays to enjoy on their stereoscopic displays. Apple made 3D films a key selling point for the Vision Pro. And now Meta has finally jumped into the game with its own library of 3D films for Quest 3 and 3S headsets, which launched alongside a new Disney+ app featuring 3D content and environments. Finally, you can enjoy Tron: Legacy as it was truly meant to be seen (sort of).

As someone who's spent dozens of hours watching 3D movies on the Apple Vision Pro, I was keen to see how Meta delivered them on significantly cheaper headsets. My big takeaway? 3D movies are an exciting new feature for existing Quest 3 and Quest 3S owners, but they're also constrained by the company's OS. It's also unclear why it took so long to make 3D movies happen on Quest headsets, or why the popular Quest 2 is left out entirely.

Meta has updated its Horizon OS to more resemble Apple's visionOS over the years, but unfortunately, it's not nearly as flexible when it comes to playing video. You can't just rescale the Disney+ window on the fly to make it larger — it has to fit into a specific spot in Meta's OS. As a front row cinema sicko, I prefer having video windows completely filling my field of view, and occasionally bleeding into my peripheral vision. The only way to achieve that on the Quest 3 is by playing 3D videos within the cinema environment, where you can choose to sit in the front, middle or back rows. It gets the job done, but it's not ideal if you want to juggle a 3D video along with other VR apps.

While it's nice to see Meta finally offering 3D movies, I genuinely wonder why it took the company so long to do so. The Quest 3 launched in the fall of 2023! There's nothing technically preventing the Quest 2 or 1 from streaming 3D either. I figure that Apple has maintained some sort of exclusivity over 3D Disney+ content since the Vision Pro launched in 2024, but that didn't prevent Meta from offering 3D films on its own. At this point, as Quest sales are on the extreme decline, it's not really much of a selling point.

Perhaps Meta just wanted to have a shiny new feature for its upcoming $1,300 VR Glasses, which is set to arrive in spring 2027. I haven't tested those yet, but I can imagine watching high quality 3D movies on a much lighter pair of glasses will be much more comfortable than strapping a Quest headset on your noggin. For now, though, it's certainly a nice feature to have if you still own a Quest 3 or 3S.

#286. Kenya Starts Countdown to End of Ebola Outbreak

The milestone is further evidence that African countries have mostly succeeded in preventing community transmission and isolating the small number of cases that have come in from Congo.

Kenya began a countdown on Wednesday to declare the end of its Ebola outbreak.

The final patient who had tested positive for the virus was discharged from the hospital on Wednesday. And the Kenyan cabinet secretary for health, Aden Duale, said at a news conference that the last 11 people being monitored for Ebola symptoms had also been discharged after completing a 21-day quarantine period.

“For the first time since this outbreak began, Kenya has no patient under treatment and no contact in quarantine,” he said.

The milestone is further evidence that countries have succeeded in containing the small number of cases, most coming in from the Democratic Republic of Congo, where the outbreak continues to spread.

Since Kenya announced its first-ever Ebola case on Oct. 6, two people have died and nine have tested positive in the country. The initial case was a Kenyan man who returned from Congo and infected others before the virus was diagnosed and he was put in isolation. He died days later.

The Kenyan authorities say that all of those who subsequently became ill were in quarantine before they developed symptoms and became infectious. As a result, there have been no instances of what experts call community transmission, when a person passes on the virus outside a medical setting.

Kenya’s outbreak will be officially declared over on Dec. 23, or 42 days from when the last case was recorded, if there are no further positive Ebola tests, the health secretary said. In all, 135 people have been monitored for signs of the virus.

The current Ebola outbreak has killed more than 5,300 people since it was first declared a global health emergency by the World Health Organization in May. But so far, the fear that the epidemic might spread unchecked across Africa has not materialized.

Uganda has recorded 20 cases and three deaths, but the outbreak was declared over in the country by August. The authorities in Nigeria last month identified a case in the capital, Abuja.

Individuals with Ebola have since been medically evacuated to several European countries. Two Americans who worked for humanitarian organizations involved in the Ebola response have also tested positive. Both were treated outside of the United States and have recovered.

Several countries have instituted travel restrictions to prevent the spread of the virus. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention instituted a requirement in November that anyone traveling by air into the United States must sign a document confirming that they have not been in Congo in the past 21 days before boarding their flight.

Kenya has also announced new rules for its citizens returning from Congo, including 21 days of quarantine and monitoring, Mr. Duale said. He gave no details of whether the quarantine would take place at home or in a medical facility.

Matthew Mpoke Bigg is the East Africa bureau chief for The New York Times, based in Nairobi, Kenya.

“Kalshi’s donation of shares to the US president’s eldest son granted him a stake in the private company when it was valued at under $2 billion, less than a tenth of its $22 billion valuation in a funding round last month,” the Financial Times article said. Kalshi is reportedly in talks for a new funding round that would value it at $65 billion.

Trump Jr. simultaneously has an interest in Polymarket via his role as a partner at 1789 Capital and as an advisor for Polymarket. The venture firm’s investment of $300 million in Polymarket was reported in August 2026, building on a smaller investment it made in Polymarket in August 2025.

“We have been discussing this internally since August when news of 1789’s investment in Polymarket became public,” a Kalshi spokesperson said. “Kalshi reached out to Don Jr. this fall to discuss his firm’s increased investment in our top competitor and his role on its advisory board… both parties understood that advising two competitors in a growing sector was complicating things for both of us.”

The Trump administration has helped prediction markets grow with a permissive regulatory approach taken by the Commodity Futures Exchange Commission (CFTC). The CFTC has also sued 11 states in attempts to block state regulation of prediction markets, and filed briefs supporting Kalshi in its own cases against individual states. Attorneys general in many states accuse Kalshi of running an illegal gambling operation.

Kalshi co-founder and CEO Tarek Mansour told MS Now in an interview last month, “I get asked a lot about Don. I mean, I think that is a fair question… We are facing sort of an optics issue, I think.”

Mansour claimed the relationship was not political, and it began as a way to market Kalshi at a time when it was much smaller and the public didn’t know much about prediction markets. “The key thing is we did it before the election. It is not a political decision, and it still isn’t. That’s the key thing,” Mansour said.

In fact, Kalshi named Trump Jr. as a strategic advisor over two months after the November 2024 presidential election. Mansour announced Trump Jr.’s appointment as strategic advisor in a post on January 13, 2025, a week before Donald Trump’s second inauguration.

Trump Jr. announced his position with Kalshi in an X post on the same day and indicated that he decided to help Kalshi on Election Day.

“On Election night at Mar-a-Lago, while biased outlets called the race a coin toss, my family and close friends used the prediction market Kalshi to know we won hours ahead of the fake news media,” Trump Jr. wrote at the time. “I immediately knew I had to contribute to their mission. Today, I am proud to announce that I am joining Kalshi as a strategic advisor.”

After Mansour’s claim that “we did it before the election,” MS Now Host Stephanie Ruhle said, “But you didn’t say after, you know what, maybe this is a conflict of interest.”

Mansour’s response suggested that Trump Jr. did not help Kalshi on regulatory matters. “We never had any policy regulatory involvement,” he said. “It was always about getting the word out about prediction markets, how they work, looking at the forecasts, making sure people understand that it’s not a poll; it’s actually probabilities. That was always our focus… we have advisors from both sides of the aisle. We have Democratic advisors; we have Republican advisors.”

Mansour said Kalshi sought to work with anyone “that can help us get the message out… anyone who likes prediction markets, and Don does like prediction markets, we wanted to work with, because we wanted to get the word out about what prediction markets are. Don has 10 million followers on Twitter… he has a huge following, and he likes the idea of going direct, tweeting.”

The Kalshi spokesperson said this week that “Don Jr. has been a great marketing consultant for us—he helped us ‘go direct’ early on, which was a big part of our growth online.”

Kalshi has also paid former US Senator Blanche Lincoln (D-Ark.) for lobbying services since 2024. As a Democratic senator in 2010, Lincoln helped write the law that now regulates prediction markets and said the US should not let event contracts be used for sports gambling. As a Kalshi lobbyist, she supports event contracts based on the results of sporting contests and opposes state regulation of prediction markets.

Trump Media and Technology Group last year announced plans for a “Truth Predict” prediction market for Truth Social, but appears to have scaled back the plan.

US Senators Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) and Jeff Merkley (D-Ore.) raised concerns about Trump Jr.’s investments in Kalshi and Polymarket in a November 9 letter to White House Counsel Will Scharf. Warren and Merkley said a recent incident of insider trading, in which a Trump teleprompter aide made over $100,000 betting on what Trump would say in prepared remarks on Kalshi’s mention markets, “should have been a wake-up call to the entire administration to enforce stronger ethics policies.”

The CFTC settled charges against the teleprompter operator, Gabriel Perez, ordering him to disgorge profits of $107,539.02 and pay a civil monetary penalty of $65,000. Warren and Merkley said that despite the incident, “the White House continues to take no meaningful action to address these problems, all while the President and his family stand to personally profit from their participation in prediction markets.”

For example, the President’s family has become even more entangled with the success of this industry. Earlier this year, Donald Trump Jr. became an advisor to Polymarket shortly before his venture capital firm made a $300 million investment in the company. Trump Jr. likewise received an equity stake in Kalshi worth $300,000 in January 2025 and also joined the company as an advisor. Just last year, Trump Media announced it was developing its own prediction market called “Truth Predict” in partnership with Crypto.com.

The CFTC reportedly is close to bringing charges against Kalshi alleging that it didn’t properly notify the agency about dozens of new event contracts, but the CFTC is also continuing its fight against states trying to regulate prediction markets more strictly. The federal agency yesterday filed a lawsuit attempting to stop Arkansas from regulating Kalshi, after suing 10 other states in similar cases.

Kalshi has lost a number of cases in which US appeals courts and district courts refused to block state gambling laws. Louisiana Attorney General Liz Murrill yesterday touted a state court victory in which a judge issued a “temporary restraining order prohibiting Kalshi, Polymarket, Coinbase, Robinhood, and other ‘prediction market’ operators from continuing their unlicensed and unregulated illegal gambling in Louisiana.”

“It’s not complicated: if you aren’t a licensed gaming operator, you aren’t legal,” Murrill wrote. “Illegal gambling is a crime and is subject to jail time and significant penalties. Judges all over the country have overwhelmingly called this activity what it is: gambling. Kalshi’s and the other operators’ attempts to make up new words to evade state gambling laws, regulators, and taxes are not fooling anyone.”

Kalshi argues that all its wagers—even ones that are functionally identical to sports bets—are event contracts, a type of swap that it says can only be regulated under federal law. Kalshi’s most significant win came against New Jersey in the US Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit, but the state is asking the Supreme Court to overturn the ruling. If the Supreme Court takes up the case, the Trump administration can be expected to weigh in on Kalshi’s behalf.

#284. Google now lets people connect Gemini to their bank accounts

This is for spending analysis and not for making purchases.

Google just announced that Gemini users can now connect the chatbot to their bank accounts via integration with Google Wallet. This is not a shopping platform in which chatbots buy stuff on command, like Meta Muse. Rather, it's being pitched as a way for users to "get more tailored responses around personal finance."

Once connected, folks can ask the chatbot questions about their accounts. This could, in theory, help save money by finding unnecessary subscriptions and the like. Google gives an example of the chatbot sussing out a price increase for a particular subscription. This information could already be gleaned by just looking at a bank statement or two, but where would be the fun in that?

Personal Intelligence in Gemini securely connects information from apps you already use to make your experience more personalized and helpful. Today, we're adding Google Wallet to the list of connected apps so you can get more tailored responses in the @GeminiApp around personal...

The company does give a couple of examples that go above and beyond what one would find by staring at a banking app. Users can ask Gemini to come up with specific budgets for predetermined time periods, complete with "dynamic charts tracking cash flow, asset allocation" and more. It can also reportedly help set up a holiday budget, and it's getting to be that time of the year.

Of course, security is a major concern here. AI chatbots are going to make mistakes every once in a while, and we would all very much like these mistakes to stay far away from our personal finances. This new toolset only lets Gemini read information from accounts and it cannot "move, manage or invest" any money. It's also a completely opt-in experience and it looks like the feature can be turned on and off rather easily via settings.

This integration is rolling out right now across web and mobile. Google says it has also partnered with the fintech company Plaid to help users "unlock deeper insights." The company didn't give any specifics here. Engadget has reached out to Google to inquire about this partnership and will update the post when we hear back.

This is just the latest news involving AI chatbots having access to our money. OpenAI recently unveiled a feature that lets its Dots agents shop on a user's behalf, joining Meta Muse. This kind of thing has already caused a headache in certain sectors of the internet. Companies like United Airlines, Delta and even Amazon have all banned AI agents from shopping on their respective platforms.

#282. Some international physical copies of GTA VI can allegedly be redeemed without opening the box

The box was already for decoration, and now maybe more so.

Less than a week before Grand Theft Auto VI is released into the wild, online reports have started to bubble up about a potentially serious issue with the retail version of the game. According to posts on ResetEra and X, the download code for the game may be visible from the outside without opening the box if you have a strong enough flashlight.

The code is visible because, rather than including a paper insert with the code or covering it with a scratch-off coating, it's reportedly printed on the inside cover of the GTA VI box. Only a few photos are currently circulating of codes illuminated through the side of the game box, so it's hard to say if this is truly a widespread issue. The ResetEra post says it may be limited to international copies of the game, because the US game box includes a backing that camouflages the code from the outside.

Fans have discovered the entire GTA 6 redeem code on the physical copy can be seen without opening the box by shining a powerful flashlight through the side at an angle. pic.twitter.com/YtN5QhC81R

— GTA 6 Countdown ⏳ (@GTAVI_Countdown) November 13, 2026

Engadget has asked Take-Two to comment on the reports and whether it's aware of any issues with GTA VI game boxes. We'll update this article if we hear back.

If the issue is widespread, it could pose a serious problem for anyone trying to buy a physical version of the game. All you need is a code, your console and an internet connection to download GTA VI, and if you're able to get those codes without buying or even opening the retail box, it could be possible to purchase a copy of the game that's already been redeemed.

All of this hinges on Take-Two and Rockstar Games' strained definition of what a "physical" version of a game is. Traditional physical releases include a disc or cartridge that, even if it doesn't include all of the game or its final version, acts as a kind of key that you need to play. And importantly, those keys can be given to someone else if they want to play. Rockstar's physical GTA VI release is just another way to purchase the digital version of the game, and because of the nature of digital game licenses, not really something you can own. If this issue does affect a large number of copies, it could also be a game you can't play, too.

#280. In elephants, the resilience of communities has its limits

In Sri Lanka, ecological change is shrinking a park’s elephant population.

Elephants live in some of the most complex and resilient societies in the animal kingdom. Individuals die or move away, calves grow up, and newcomers join, but the local communities as a whole usually hold together. Long-term studies of African savanna elephants have shown that, even after poaching or other spikes in deaths, survivors tend to rebuild their lost connections over time. But a new study suggests that resilience may not keep the community intact when steady losses accumulate over time.

Anastasia Madsen, a behavioral ecologist at the University of California, San Diego, and her colleagues analyzed 12 years of observations of wild Asian elephants in Sri Lanka. They found that, once losses began to outpace newcomers, the elephants’ society didn’t just get smaller. It fractured.

Udawalawe National Park in southern Sri Lanka was created in the 1970s around a reservoir on one of the country’s four major rivers. “Before that, it was basically a tropical deciduous forest,” said Shermin de Silva, an ecologist at UC San Diego and senior author of the study (de Silva started the Udawalawe Elephant Research Project in 2006).

The reservoir had brought farming and settlers, some of whom lived inside what later became the park. When they were moved out, the land they had cleared for their crops turned into grassland that, according to de Silva, looks very much like an African savanna. With far more food than the original forest offered, the park became famous for its big elephant herds.

But the savanna was only a stage in the forest’s comeback. Without fires and clearing, invasive plants like lantana took over. “In some places, they’re like an impenetrable thicket. Not even elephants really like to go there because there’s no food for them,” de Silva said. This is a problem because elephants tend to move where the grass is. A park with less forage, the team says, stops attracting elephants from outside while, due to limited food, fewer calves reach adulthood.

Her team watched this transformation from a vehicle, driving random routes along the park’s roads about three days a week. The team identified elephants mostly by their floppy ears, which collect holes, tears, and cuts with age. “Their ears are very individually distinct, so it’s like a fingerprint,” de Silva said. Between 2007 and 2018, the team identified 443 adult and subadult females that roamed the park.

The team focused on females because males lead more solitary lives, ranging widely in search of mates. Females live in a so-called fission-fusion society. “It’s kind of similar to humans,” Madsen said. “You might have your work friends, you might have your family, and you’re not always with those same individuals at the same time, but they’re all part of your social network.” Unlike African savanna elephants, where families follow older matriarchs, Asian elephants move more independently. This may be because Asia’s rainy environment ensures that food and water are rarely far apart and usually easier to find, so following an experienced individual brings less benefit.

Madsen reconstructed the elephants’ social network for each year, measuring the strength of each relationship by how often two animals were seen together. “You can think of it as a probability that you will see those two individuals together,” she explains. An algorithm commonly used for analyzing human social networks then picked out communities—clusters of elephants with many strong ties to one another.

For the first few years of the study, these communities seemed to hold very well. But then cracks started to appear.

Around 2012, the elephants that died or left began to outnumber the newcomers. By 2018, about 69 percent of the females seen at the start of the study were gone, even though Asian elephants can live 60 years or more. Soon after the balance tipped, communities grew smaller, more numerous and more isolated from each other. “As individuals are being lost from the network, there’s nobody filling it back in,” de Silva says.

A shrinking population naturally makes for a smaller network, so the team checked whether this was just a numbers game. They looked at a population of 59 elephants seen every year of the study, which the team referred to as residents. “If it was just an artifact of the data, those core individuals should have fairly consistent relationships. But that’s not what we saw,” Madsen said. “They were changing their relationships with each other, even though they were still there.” Among these residents, the fracturing came one to three years after the decline began.

Madsen suspects some of the lost elephants were the glue holding groups together. “If you go to a city hall meeting, it’s maybe the same group of people that constantly comes in. You’re not friends with every single other person in that group, though,” she said. “There could have been some key individuals that were lost that were holding these subgroups together.”

The first ties to go were the weakest, the casual acquaintances. These weak relationships, Madsen says, could represent animals that merely tolerate each other: “Tolerance is really important in a society, especially in large animals. If they get into conflict, it could be serious.” These weak ties, researchers argue, may still be important because they can carry information between communities.

De Silva links this fracturing to the disappearing grass. “As the competition becomes more severe, your social sphere sort of shrinks,” she said. “It’s as if they’re emphasizing the quality over the quantity of the relationships.”

Before the breakup, some network statistics fluctuated from year to year, which complex systems scientists call flickering. “If you have a light bulb that’s right on the edge of dying, it might start flickering,” Madsen said. “It’s on, it’s off, it’s on, it’s off. And then suddenly it dies.” Spotted early, she argues, such signals could serve as an early warning sign. “Hopefully, park managers and conservationists can detect that and intervene before the population collapses,” Madsen said.

The challenge is that this detection requires long-term data, and we don’t have that for many populations. What worries the researchers most is that, without early detection, we may only recognize problems after it’s too late. “If you are taking the boards out of a ship and you’re not replacing them, the ship starts to take on water,” Madsen said. “There was a point of no return where there was structural damage, but you might not have noticed it until later.”

The study’s data end in 2018, but de Silva says things haven’t improved. Her team predicted a decline in the park’s elephant population in 2019, and it now appears to be happening faster than expected. “They don’t appear to be breeding very well. A lot of the calves are not surviving,” she said. Many adult females die in their 30s and 40s, and they should be living roughly twice as long.

Part of the reason they die so young is conflict with humans. Female Asian elephants have no tusks, so ivory poaching isn’t the main threat. Instead, elephants get shot when they raid crops or startle people. Occasionally, de Silva says, they also eat explosives hidden in pumpkins some locals place to get rid of wild boars. But the bigger problem, she says, is that elephants can’t survive in the protected areas alone.

More than half of Asian elephant populations live in poor-quality habitats, and the species has lost nearly two-thirds of its likely historic range. “Management really has to be about entire landscapes,” de Silva said. “Roads and infrastructure, irrigation canals—those are the things that are hurting the elephants. It’s not really a wildlife management problem.” Her team now works with farmers outside the park, testing crops elephants are less likely to damage. “Their long-term persistence really depends on people and elephants being able to share the space safely,” she said.

Science, 2026. DOI: 10.1126/science.aef6714

#279. Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out the New York Times

Some VIP Twitter users woke up on Saturday expecting to have lost their coveted blue verification check marks in a previously announced purge by Elon Musk. Instead, Twitter appeared to target a single account from a major publication Musk dislikes and changed the language on its site in a way that obscures why users are verified.

Twitter had said it would “begin winding down” blue checks granted under its old verification system — which emphasized protecting high-profile users at risk of impersonation — on April 1. In order to stay verified, Musk said, users would have to pay $8 per month to join the platform’s Twitter Blue subscription service, which has allowed accounts to pay for verification since December.

But one high-profile account did lose its blue check over the weekend: the main account for the New York Times, which had previously told CNN it would not pay for verification.

William Shatner is mad he may have to pay to keep his blue Twitter checkmark. Elon Musk says too bad

After an account that often engages with Musk posted a meme this weekend about the Times declining to pay for verification, Musk responded in a tweet saying, “Oh ok, we’ll take it off then.” Musk then lashed out at the Times — just the latest instance of the billionaire slamming journalists or media outlets — in a series of tweets that claimed the outlet’s coverage is boring and “propaganda.”

The weekend moves are just the latest example of Twitter creating confusion and whiplash for users over feature changes — and in this case, not just any users, but many of the most high-profile accounts that have long been a key selling point for the platform. It also highlights how Musk often appears to guide decisions about the platform more by whims than by policy.

Although the New York Times’ main account lost its blue check, its other accounts, such as those for its arts, travel and books content, remained verified. (It’s not clear why the New York Times doesn’t have a gold “organizations” check mark, like accounts for other news outlets, including the Associated Press and the Washington Post.) After its blue check was removed, a spokesperson for the New York Times reiterated to CNN that it does not plan to pay for verification.

Twitter, which laid off most of its public relations staff last fall, did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In a separate puzzling move, Twitter’s blue bird logo at the top of the site was replaced on Monday with doge, the meme representing the cryptocurrency dogecoin, which Musk has promoted. The price of dogecoin shot up 20% on Monday.

Musk has been threatening to take away “legacy” blue check marks from users verified under Twitter’s old system since shortly after he bought Twitter last fall.

In early November, Twitter launched the option for people paying for its Twitter Blue subscription service to receive blue checks. The program was quickly put on pause after being plagued by a wave of celebrity and corporate impersonators, and was relaunched in December.

Twitter also rolled out a color-coded verification system with differently colored marks for companies and government entities, but Musk continued to say that individual users would eventually have to pay for blue checks.

In the days leading up to the blue check purge that wasn’t, prominent users such as actor William Shatner and anti-bullying activist Monica Lewinksy pushed back against the idea that, as power users that draw attention to the site, they should have to pay for a feature that keeps them safe from impersonation.

By muddying the reason accounts are verified, the new label could risk making it easier for people to scam or impersonate high-profile users. Experts in inauthentic behavior have also said it’s not clear that reserving verification for paid users will reduce the number of bots on the site, an issue Musk has raised on and off over the past year.

Musk, for his part, has previously presented changes to Twitter’s verification system as a way of “treating everyone equally.”

“There shouldn’t be a different standard for celebrities,” he said in a tweet last week. The paid feature could also drive revenue, which could help Musk, who is on the hook for significant debt after buying Twitter for $44 billion.

Musk last week also said that starting on April 15, only verified accounts would be recommended in users’ “For You” feeds alongside the accounts they follow.

–CNN’s Oliver Darcy contributed to this report.

#278. FAA allows flights to resume after hours-long ground stop across the US

Transport secretary Sean Duffy blamed the USA's 'incredibly old air traffic control system.'

The Federal Aviation Administration has lifted a nationwide ground stop in the US that brought all flights to a halt for hours, starting in the afternoon of November 13. Hundreds of flights were canceled, thousands that were already flying had been diverted and millions of passengers were stranded as the ground stop went on. After the FAA lifted the suspension at 8:15PM Eastern time, transportation secretary Sean Duffy held a press conference, blaming old equipment — and the Congress' unwillingness to fund their upgrades — for what happened.

Duffy explained that the USA's "incredibly old air traffic control system" malfunctioned. The US still uses TDM telecom, which is a legacy analog telecommunications technology, to transmit information between facilities. Duffy said that its routers got "overloaded with data," and by 3:36PM on the 13th, they started transmitting inconsistent information. The secretary said the equipment that had already been replaced weren't affected, only the old ones. He also clarified that the US didn't lose all communications and radar, but the FAA still decided to ground flights out of an abundance of caution.

"It's going to continue to fail," Duffy told reporters about the country's air traffic control system, according to the New York Times. "How many times does this have to happen for Congress to give the money to fix it?" The FAA has only issued ground stops thrice this century, and this was the second longest after the 9/11 attacks. While the Congress has already given the FAA $12.5 billion to modernize the country's air traffic facilities, Duffy has been trying to secure $30 billion more.

Markwayne Mullin, the secretary of Homeland Security, appeared with Duffy at the conference and said that there was no indication that the communication issues were caused by a cyberattack. Still, he said his agency was "still looking," and Homeland Security is "not going to rule it out." In addition, Mullin clarified that the incident wasn't related to the lengthy outage that took out Microsoft's services for over 24 hours.

#277. Alexander Darchiev, Russia’s Ambassador to the U.S., Dies at 66

The death of Alexander Darchiev was announced by Russia’s foreign ministry. He was appointed to his post in Washington in 2025, as relations between the Kremlin and the White House warmed.

Russia’s ambassador to the United States, Alexander Darchiev, who served during a rapprochement between the Kremlin and Washington, has died, the Russian foreign ministry said Wednesday. He was 66.

Mr. Darchiev died in Moscow of a “chronic illness,” Maria Zakharova, spokeswoman for the ministry, said in a statement published by Tass, the Russian state news agency.

“We have lost an outstanding diplomat, an experienced leader, a kind colleague and a mentor,” the ministry said in its statement. It described Mr. Darchiev as a “distinguished expert on the United States” who had “served in one of the most demanding areas of Russian foreign policy.”

Mr. Darchiev had been in the diplomatic service since 1992, and he was appointed as ambassador to the United States by President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia in March 2025.

His appointment came as President Trump sought to broker a cease-fire agreement to halt Russia’s war against Ukraine, which began in 2022. Mr. Darchiev’s term coincided with a thaw in relations between the Kremlin and Washington as U.S. policy shifted from staunch support for Ukraine under the Biden administration to pressure on Kyiv to make a deal with Russia.

In February last year, Mr. Darchiev led a Russian delegation meeting with U.S. officials in Istanbul about the operations of the two nations’ diplomatic missions. At the time he was director of the North Atlantic Department, a section of the Russian foreign ministry that oversees relations with Canada, Ireland, Britain and the United States.

“The dialogue on irritants has been ongoing, and certain improvements have been achieved,” Mr. Darchiev said last year in an interview with the Russian newspaper Kommersant in which he discussed frictions between the United States and Russia.

But he said the United States was tying discussions on certain issues, like Russian access to seized diplomatic properties in the United States, “with a settlement in Ukraine that suits its interests,” which he objected to.

Talks on ending the war in Ukraine, which had been stalled for much of this year, resumed this week in Abu Dhabi, the United Arab Emirates, after a push by the Trump administration that began in September. The discussions are not expected to yield fruit anytime soon.

It is not clear if Mr. Darchiev was involved in those talks.

Mr. Darchiev, who was born in 1960, graduated from Moscow State University in 1983. Information about his survivors was not immediately available.

Mr. Darchiev’s appointment to Washington as ambassador to the United States was not his first stint in the country or the region. He served as Russia’s ambassador to Canada from 2014 to 2020 and worked at the Russian Embassy in the United States from 2005 to 2010, according to a biography on the embassy’s website.

In the intervening years, Mr. Darchiev directed the North America Department and, briefly, the foreign ministry’s North Atlantic department, before serving in what proved to be his final role.

Ephrat Livni is a Times reporter covering news around the world. She is based in New York.

#275. FAA expands AI advice to manage air traffic congestion across entire US airspace

Airlines praised the AI tool, but the air traffic controllers’ union wasn’t involved.

The US government has expanded the use of an AI tool for advising air traffic controllers to assist in managing air traffic across the entire national airspace system. Airline executives have voiced support for the rollout, but the US labor union representing air traffic controllers has said it was not involved in designing or testing the AI-driven system.

The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) says the $875 million SMART system has helped reroute 180 flights since being implemented in airspace over Washington, DC, on September 21. The agency claims that this saved an average of 10 minutes per flight and almost 80 gallons of fuel per flight—and it is apparently hoping such results can be replicated nationwide through the expanded deployment.

“After a successful initial rollout to the National Capital Region in September, the FAA will now use SMART in the airspace of all 22 centers across the country to reduce delays, reduce fuel consumption and improve the travel experience for the flying public,” according to the agency statement posted on November 10.

The FAA fact sheet describes SMART as a cloud-based system that combines information about “weather patterns, flight paths, traffic flow and controller staffing metrics” to predict air traffic patterns. It is designed to make scheduling recommendations, identify optimal flight routes, and determine the best order of arrivals for aircraft.

However, a human team of FAA specialists based in DC is ultimately responsible for validating the SMART system recommendations before passing them along to air traffic control facilities across the country, according to the agency.

Furthermore, the AI-powered SMART system “cannot be used to replace the critical role air traffic controllers play or take over control of an aircraft,” the FAA clarified in the September 21 announcement about the initial deployment. Local air traffic controllers can decide whether or not to accept the SMART system recommendations.

The previous FAA announcement included supportive statements from Delta Air Lines CEO Ed Bastian and American Airlines CEO Robert Isom.

But the National Air Traffic Controllers Association, which represents more than 15,000 members, also told Flying magazine that it was not consulted or involved in the “design, testing, or implementation of SMART and therefore cannot speak to its effect on the air traffic control workforce.”

Following the initial deployment in airspace above DC, the FAA expanded the SMART system’s coverage to air route traffic control centers in Atlanta along with Jacksonville and Miami in Florida, according to The Dallas Morning News.

The FAA also shared an example with The Dallas Morning News about how SMART recommended a shorter and faster route for a cargo flight arriving at Dallas-Fort Worth International Airport, although the newspaper noted that the agency did not identify the flight involved and has not provided information about how SMART may have assisted passenger flight traffic.

Ars has previously covered the SMART system’s development and lingering questions about how it works, as well as the air traffic controller shortage and other challenges facing the FAA.

#274. This boiled bag of offal is banned in the US. In Scotland it's a fine-dining treat

Anthony Bourdain loved haggis. But even the late, great American chef, writer and television host recognized that Scotland’s national dish, with its “sinister sheep parts” wrapped in a shroud of mystery and half-invented history, could be a hard sell.

“Don’t let them tell you otherwise, that’s really one of life’s great pleasures,” Bourdain said on one of his gastro-curious pilgrimages to Glasgow. “There is no more unfairly reviled food on Earth than the haggis.”

A mash-up of diced lung, liver and heart mixed with oatmeal, beef suet, onion and assorted spices, haggis was traditionally made by stuffing these raw ingredients into the stomach of a recently slain sheep and boiling the lot to a state of palatability.

Instagrammable is not the word that immediately comes to mind. In our 21st-century world, where “clean” eating and processed pap overlap, haggis can seem like an “Outlander”-style outlier from another age.

Yet, by some alchemy, once cooked to its required “warm-reekin’ (steaming)” state, it adds up to much more than the sum of its modest parts. It’s offaly charm has kept nose-to-tail eating alive among a younger generation of Scots that has largely turned its back on the tripe, liver and kidneys their predecessors enjoyed (or endured).

Carefully prepared, haggis tastes both oaty and meaty; it is dark and crumbly, a little crispy at the edges but still moist; earthy but also savory and spicy; deep-tasting and profoundly warming, the perfect foil for its traditional garnish of floury mashed potatoes and orange bashed turnip.

“It’s like a cuddle for the stomach,” says Nicola Turner, a 35-year-old office administrator from Helensburgh, a town on western Scotland’s Firth of Clyde.

For children of the 1960s and ’70s, like crime novelist Ian Rankin, haggis meals were a choice between the classic meat-and-two-veg plate and the battered and deep-fried, chip-shop iteration loved by both his friend Bourdain and his quintessentially Scottish detective character, Inspector John Rebus.

Now myriad other treatments have blossomed.

“I’m pretty sure the first time I dined with AB in Edinburgh we had haggis in filo pastry with a jam-style – maybe blackcurrant – sauce,” Rankin recalled. “He was a big fan of haggis and of chip shops. Rebus will have enjoyed the occasional haggis supper from his local chip shop. He was definitely a fan, as am I.”

“It is all about the spicing and the texture,” says the Scottish food writer, novelist and cook Sue Lawrence, a champion of haggis’ adaptability for use in other dishes. “If you didn’t know what was in it, you wouldn’t think ‘oh that tastes of liver or whatever.’ It is all nicely chopped up and the oatmeal gives it a lovely texture. It could easily be a nice, big mince dish.”

These US restaurants and chefs are James Beard Award finalists

Lawrence uses haggis as an alternative to beef and pork ragù in lasagna and in her pastilla, a version of the North African dish in which a hand-made haggis from the Isle of Mull substitutes for the traditional poultry or seafood filling. The filo pastry savory is flavored with the spice blend ras el hanout, apricots, chile, orange zest and almonds before being sprinkled with cinnamon and icing sugar.

Such cultural crossovers serve as a reminder that haggis could easily be a dish with nothing specifically Scottish about it at all. Records of similar quick and portable preparations of the fast-perishing innards of sheep and other animals date back to ancient Rome and Greece.

Haggis-like combinations of offal and grains are part of the culinary history of several countries. Spain has chireta, Romania drob and Sweden polsa, while chaudin, or ponce, is a rice and meat-stuffed pig stomach that is a staple of Cajun cooking.

In neighboring England, recipes for “hagese,” “hagws of a schepe,” “haggas” or “haggus” pop up in recipe books published between the 15th and 17th centuries, probably preceding written records north of the border.

Etymological evidence points to the term “haggis” having its roots in Old Norse, suggesting an early version of an oat-and-offal sausage might have arrived in Britain and Ireland on a Viking longboat.

But ever since it was first optioned by the poet Robert Burns in the late 1700s, the haggis backstory has been monopolized by Scotland and the Scots, sometimes mischievously.

It is, according to the kind of lore that Burns engendered, the dish a doughty Highlander would carry with him as he drove cattle through the glens to the markets of the central belt or the perfect picnic for a whisky smuggler plying his illicit trade by moonlight.

From such romantic notions it was a short step to turning the haggis into a wee wild beastie, one with longer legs on one side that was thus condemned to run round and round whichever hill it lived on. In 2003, a poll of American tourists in Scotland found that one in three of them believed they might encounter such a confused creature on a Caledonian vacation.

Bourdain, a native New Yorker, may have qualified as haggis’ biggest admirer since Burns, but his compatriots at the US Department of Agriculture remain unconverted to offal-filled paunch. Haggis imports into the United States were prohibited in 1971 as part of a ban on the consumption of all livestock lungs. Authentic versions of old school haggis remain culinary contraband in the US, as hard to lay your hands on as Cuban cigars.

Across the rest of the world, it’s a different story. According to leading producer Simon Howie, haggis is more widely appreciated and consumed now than it has been since Burns improvised his “Address to a Haggis” for the entertainment of well-to-do Edinburgh acquaintances.

Firmly tongue-in-cheek, the poem lauds the “Great chieftain o’ the pudding race,” as exactly the kind of unpretentious, hearty fare required to nourish a nation of braveheart warriors.

In comparison to the enfeebling foreign muck enjoyed by the capital’s claret-quaffing elites of the time – the olio, fricassée or ragoût that would “sicken a sow” – Burns urges his readers to wonder at the magical impact of haggis on his fellow sons of Scotland’s soil.

As the English translation of the original Scots language version puts it:

These days synthetic casings have largely replaced stomach but ovine and porcine innards remain at the core of most of the haggis produced in its homeland, said Howie, who estimates that his company Simon Howie Butchers, accounts for around 60% of the roughly two million haggises produced every year.

For Howie, versatility, value for money and convenience explain why this staple of the Scottish larder is thriving. Typically haggis retails in Scotland, which accounts for half of global consumption by volume, for around £6, or $7.70 per kilogram ($3.36/pound). That’s around half the price of less expensive cuts of beef or a third of the price of Scotch lamb while enjoying a fairly similar nutritional and calorific profile.

“You can give your kids a meal that is not full of things you don’t want to feed them – for a few pounds you can feed three strapping lads,” Howie said.

“From a kitchen perspective, it is very simple because when it leaves our factory it is already cooked. So when you or a restaurant owner gets it into the kitchen all you have to do is heat it up to be piping hot. It couldn’t be more basic: a student with no cooking skills or a Michelin-starred chef do exactly the same thing to put it out on the plate.”

Its texture means haggis can also be usefully deployed in fine dining alongside leaner meat like venison or as a stuffing for poultry and game birds. Its spicy intensity means it is also finding uses in canapés and as a crouton-borne garnish for soups.

Buoyant sales are also underpinned by the increasing consumption of haggis in forms inspired by Scotland’s ethnic minorities.

Glasgow’s Sikh community pioneered haggis pakora in the 1990s and samosas, spring rolls and quesadillas have followed in its wake, often using a vegetarian version of the protein in which the offal is replaced by a mix of vegetables, pulses and mushrooms.

Such dishes are more than culinary twists. They are badges of belonging, and an indication that, two centuries after Burns grabbed it for the nation, haggis is as intimately entwined with Scots identity as ever.

Just ask Ross O’Cinneide, a promising 14-year-old fly-half in the junior section of Stirling County rugby club.

“Most of my friends and I like haggis,” he says. “Mum makes it for us sometimes after rugby and it’s got a very nice warming feeling. And it’s nice because it’s purely Scottish.”

#273. Outage at HR platform Workday locks millions of employees out of their account

The company placed its system in maintenance mode after discovering an issue.

A widespread Workday outage on the evening of November 13 left employees unable to clock in and out, access their pay info, request for time off or file for benefits. According to San Francisco Chronicle, the human resources platform said it placed some of its systems into maintenance mode to fix an issue it discovered during testing. Specifically, the problem left some information inconsistent, and the company said it could cause errors when users try to access their accounts or complete tasks within its system. There was a spike in reports at Downdetector at around 5:19PM Eastern time, though it has tapered off since then.

It's not quite clear if the outage affected all of Workday's customers. But the company said 11,500 organizations around the world use it for various HR tasks. Its clients include Target, Walmart, Charles Schwab, Nasdaq, Netflix and even the US Department of Energy and NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory. The company has apologized for the disruption and said that it's looking for the best way to fix the problem.

#272. A.I. and Market Whiplash

A Times reporter who covers financial markets helps us make sense of tech turbulence.

Last week’s midterm elections were perhaps the first major elections fought at least in part over issues related to artificial intelligence. This year saw record donations from tech executives to tech-friendly candidates. And in some cases, A.I. was actually on the ballot: In local ballot initiatives across the country, people voted to limit or prevent the development of data centers. Many newly elected Democrats have promised A.I. regulation.

If A.I. has surged to the forefront of U.S. politics, it’s in part because A.I. is at the forefront of the U.S. economy. And stock markets around the world rise and fall with A.I. shares, particularly the multitrillion-dollar behemoths based in Silicon Valley. I spoke to my colleague Joe Rennison, who covers financial markets, about the dramatic swings in tech stocks following the midterms and what they mean — for Americans, and for the rest of us.

So, Joe. We’ve seen some serious volatility in the stock market after the midterms. First markets rallied, then they had their worst day in nearly two years. This week, as Nvidia stock plunged because of the Jensen Huang affair, the rest of the market bounced back.

What’s going on, and how much of all this is down to politics?

Certainly some of it is down to politics. The initial rally after the midterms came in part from investors who had parked their cash on the sidelines, in case of some unexpected event — say, a contested election. They bought back in when it became evident we were going to get a clear outcome.

The plunge the next day, though, came after some weaker than expected financial reports from a couple of A.I. companies, and after investors began processing what Democratic control of Congress and some Democratic wins at the state level might actually mean for the A.I. industry.

#271. Millions of Americans are not taking their GLP-1 drugs as prescribed

Cost and access barriers are pushing people to modified use and unapproved versions.

GLP-1 medications are everywhere these days. The peptide-based drugs are both highly effective and wildly popular for diabetes, weight loss and a growing range of connected chronic health conditions, including cardiovascular disease and obstructive sleep apnea. But, amid the boom, the latest data suggests millions of Americans have turned to unapproved versions and are using the potent drugs in ways doctors never intended, raising questions about what that real-world use means for safety and efficacy.

In a study published this week, researchers at the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention broke down survey data of GLP-1 use collected between July and August of 2025. Based on responses from over 8,000 US adults, the researchers estimate that about one in 10 US adults (9.8 percent) used a glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist, aka GLP-1 medication, in 2025. That’s around 27 million adults. The study was published in the CDC’s Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report.

Among GLP-1 users, nearly half (44 percent) reported using their GLP-1 in a way that was different from how it was prescribed. Users skipped doses, delayed getting refills, and used smaller doses than prescribed, among other changes. More than half of the people modifying how they used the drug said the changes were because of cost or availability reasons. Cost and access barriers were most pronounced among younger GLP-1 users. Fifty-eight percent of adults 14­–39 years old who modified their use cited cost as a barrier, compared with just 20 percent of adults 65 or older who said the same.

Modified use of GLP-1 medications was particularly common among those using compounded versions of GLP-1 medications. Among all GLP-1 users, at least 23 percent used a compounded version; an additional 9 percent said they didn’t know whether their GLP-1 was compounded or not.

These versions of GLP-1 drugs are made by compounding pharmacies and are intended to mimic a medication approved by the Food and Drug Administration, such as Wegovy or Ozempic. But compounded drugs themselves are not approved by the FDA; the regulatory agency does not review their safety, effectiveness, or quality before they’re sold. While compounded drugs are considered more accessible and affordable, they generally have higher rates of preparation errors, contamination, and other manufacturing problems compared to non-compounded drugs.

Among people who used these unapproved, potentially riskier GLP-1s, 62 percent were not taking them as prescribed. That’s compared to just 36 percent of GLP-1 users who took brand-name drugs.

The potential risks of taking unapproved GLP-1 drugs at all or taking them in unapproved ways may be missed by users, the CDC researchers note. A 2025 study assessing websites selling compounded GLP-1 medications found that nearly 40 percent of sites stated or implied the drugs were FDA-approved, making it harder for consumers to differentiate the compounded versions from FDA-approved ones. Further, nearly 80 percent of sites used brand names in their advertising, which could further mislead consumers. The researchers noted that using brand names violated FDA regulatory standards.

“Given the substantial use of compounded GLP-1 medications, understanding the safety and quality of compounded GLP-1 medications is an important public health consideration,” the authors conclude. They also call for more research on the common modifications people are using, such as dose and frequency adjustments, saying the data on the safety and effectiveness of these real-world practices “could help guide the development and implementation of clinical guidelines and support appropriate medication use.”

#269. Kwame Brathwaite, photographer of 'Black is Beautiful' movement, dies at 85

Editor’s Note:  This article was originally published by The Art Newspaper, an editorial partner of CNN Style.

Kwame Brathwaite, the pioneering activist and photographer whose work helped define the aesthetics of the “Black is Beautiful” movement of the 1960s and beyond, died on April 1, aged 85.

His son, Kwame Brathwaite, Jr, announced his father’s death in an Instagram post that read in part, “I am deeply saddened to share that my Baba, the patriarch of our family, our rock and my hero has transitioned.”

Brathwaite’s work has been the subject of resurgent interest from curators, historians and collectors in recent years, and his first major institutional retrospective, which was organized by the Aperture Foundation, made its debut in 2019 at the Skirball Cultural Center in Los Angeles before touring the country.

Brathwaite was born in 1938 to Barbadian immigrants, in what he referred to as “the People’s Republic of Brooklyn” in New York, though his family moved from there to Harlem and then to the South Bronx when Brathwaite was 5 years old. He attended the School of Industrial Art (now the High School of Art and Design) and, according to profiles of Brathwaite in T Magazine and Vice, was drawn to photography by two moments. The first was in August of 1955, when a 17-year-old Brathwaite encountered David Jackson’s haunting photograph of a brutalized Emmett Till in his open casket. The second was in 1956, when — after he and his brother Elombe co-founded the African Jazz Arts Society and Studios (AJASS) — Brathwaite saw a young man taking photos in a dark jazz club without the use of a flash, and his mind became alight with possibility.

Using a Hasselblad medium-format camera, Brathwaite attempted to do the same, learning to work with limited light in a manner that enhanced the visual narrative of his imagery. He would soon also develop a darkroom technique that enriched and deepened how Black skin would appear in his photography, honing the practice in a small darkroom in his Harlem apartment. Brathwaite went on to photograph jazz legends performing throughout the 1950s and ’60s, including Miles Davis, John Coltrane, Thelonious Monk and others.

“You want to get the feeling, the mood that you’re experiencing when they’re playing,” Brathwaite told Aperture Magazine in 2017. “That’s the thing. You want to capture that.”

A 106-year-old from the Philippines is Vogue's oldest ever cover model

By the early 1960s, alongside the rest of AJASS, Brathwaite began using his photography and organizing prowess to consciously push back against whitewashed, Eurocentric beauty standards. The group came up with the concept of the Grandassa Models, young Black women whom Brathwaite would photograph, celebrating and accentuating their features. In 1962, AJASS organized “Naturally ’62”, a fashion show held in a Harlem club called the Purple Manor and featuring the models. The show would go on to be held regularly until 1992. In 1966, Brathwaite married his wife Sikolo, a Grandassa Model whom he had met on the street the year prior when he asked if he could take her portrait. The two remained married for the rest of Brathwaite’s life.

By the 1970s, Brathwaite’s focus on jazz shifted to other forms of popular Black music. In 1974, he traveled to Africa with the Jackson Five to document their tour, also photographing the historic “Rumble in the Jungle” boxing match between Muhammad Ali and George Foreman in what’s now the Democratic Republic of Congo that same year. Commissions in this era also saw Brathwaite photographing Nina Simone, Stevie Wonder, Sly and the Family Stone, Bob Marley and other music legends.

Creating for community: The Black designers placing identity and heritage at the center of their brands

Throughout the ensuing decades, Brathwaite continued to explore and develop his mode of photography, all through the lens of the “Black is Beautiful” ethos. In 2016, Brathwaite joined the roster of Philip Martin Gallery in Los Angeles, and he was continuing to photograph commissions as recently as 2018, when he shot artist and stylist Joanne Petit-Frère for The New Yorker.

T Magazine’s 2021 profile, published on the occasion of Brathwaite’s retrospective traveling to the Blanton Museum of Art in Austin, Texas, noted that the photographer’s health was failing such that he was unable to be interviewed for the article. A separate exhibition, “Kwame Brathwaite: Things Well Worth Waiting For,” is currently on view at the Art Institute of Chicago, where it will remain until July 24.

Top image: Kwame Brathwaite, “Untitled (Sikolo Brathwaite, Orange Portrait),” 1968

#268. FAA has lifted its third-ever nationwide ground stop

Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy blamed obsolete equipment for the outage.

The Federal Administration lifted its third-ever nationwide ground stop after several hours Friday evening. Domestic flights were cleared to begin taking off at 8:35 pm EST. FAA Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy cautioned that, barring massive system updates, there will be “more of these” ahead.

“Today’s nationwide ground stop happened because our air traffic control system is operating on DECADES-OLD analog equipment that simply cannot compete with the demands of the 21st century,” Duffy posted on X.

Over a million travelers have been affected by the delays, with some airlines cancelling flights for the remainder of Friday. “Due to the ongoing ground stop, Air Canada has cancelled all flights to and from the United States for the remainder of the evening,” the airline said.

The stoppage began at 3:36 pm EST Friday afternoon, days after expanding use of an AI tool, when the Federal Aviation Administration issued a nationwide ground stop, halting departures from all US airports. Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy announced the ground stop on X, citing a “communication issue impacting all Air Route Traffic Control Centers.” Duffy promised updates as more information is known and said FAA staff was troubleshooting the problem.

Department of Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said that the ground stop was not prompted by a security threat. “This is a telecommunications outage out of the FAA. Out of an abundance of caution, we have grounded all of the flights,” Mullin said. “There’s no indication as of yet that this is a nefarious attack against us.”

Mullin had said the FAA would handle incoming international flights on a case-by-case basis.

This was only the third nationwide ground stop issued by the FAA. The first, and most memorable, came during the attacks of September 11, 2001, when the FAA stopped all civilian departures nationwide and subsequently ordered all airborne aircraft to land. Commercial flights did not take off again until September 13. Flights were also grounded for less than two hours the morning of January 11, 2023, after its Notice to Air Missions system suffered an outage.

Unfortunately for travelers, it turned out to be a lengthy ground stop. Airlines were quick to cancel flights scheduled to leave later in the day, with American Airlines warning that disruptions were expected for the rest of the day.

“This is going to take some time,” Mullin said. “Flights are accounted for, and there should be no fear to the public at all.”

This story was updated at 10:03 pm EST to report the ground stop was lifted.

#267. Court in UK Decriminalizes Same-Sex Relations in Trinidad and Tobago

The ruling, which could affect similar laws in other British Commonwealth countries, has spotlighted a legal system still intertwined with a former colonial power.

Prior Beharry reported from Port of Spain, Trinidad and Tobago.

A ruling by a British-based court has invalidated laws criminalizing homosexuality in Trinidad and Tobago, setting a precedent for those challenging similar laws in other nations in the Caribbean and possibly beyond.

The ruling was handed down on Tuesday by the Judicial Committee of the Privy Council in London, which serves as the highest court of appeal for dozens of crown dependencies, overseas territories and former British colonies.

Five British judges unanimously upheld a 2018 decision by a lower court in Trinidad that had found two sections of the Caribbean country’s 1986 law targeting private, consensual same-sex relations to be unconstitutional. An appeals court in Trinidad had reversed the lower-court decision in 2025.

Trinidad’s attorney general had argued that the law preserved traditional family values in the country, and had leaned on a legal mechanism called “savings provisions,” which kept many laws from the British colonial era.

The decision delivered a hard-fought victory to advocates for L.G.B.T.Q. rights in Trinidad. Jason Jones, an L.G.B.T.Q. activist who had been a key plaintiff against the law since 2015, called the ruling a “vindication.” He said it would provide hope to young L.G.B.T.Q. people in his country who are living in fear of legal and societal discrimination.

Still, Mr. Jones noted ruefully, to challenge the colonial-era law, his lawyers had to seek intervention from a court based in Britain — decades after Trinidad gained independence, in 1962.

#266. The smallest version of Camp Snap’s retro digital camcorder fits on your keychain

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Camp Snap CS-8 Micro is just three inches long and adds a tiny LCD screen that’s only used as a viewfinder.

The Camp Snap CS-8 Micro is just three inches long and adds a tiny LCD screen that’s only used as a viewfinder.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Last week, Camp Snap launched a miniaturized version of its screen-free CS-8 video camera that was half the size of the original with a smaller grip designed to be easier for kids to hold. But it turns out the company wasn’t done shrinking its hardware. Camp Snap’s new CS-8 Micro is even smaller than the CS-8 Mini. It’s around three inches long and weighs just 1.5 ounces so you can hang it from your keys and, like its larger predecessors, it can capture video using one of five built-in filters.

The CS-8 Micro will be available starting on November 17th at 11AM ET through Camp Snap’s online store, Target, and Urban Outfitters for $39.99, and similar to the collectible Kodak Charmera, the Micro is being sold in sealed mystery boxes. Colors like hot pink, lilac, lime, and orange will be easier to find, while vintage classic (black) and glow in the dark will be much rarer.

Unlike the original CS-8 and CS-8 Mini, the new Micro does include an LCD display, but it’s only used as a viewfinder. You can’t watch, edit, or delete anything you’ve captured on the camera itself. You need to transfer the videos to a phone or computer using a USB-C cable to enjoy them, or move its included 4GB microSD into a card reader.

As Camp Snap has been progressively shrinking its retro camcorder, it’s also reduced its resolution. The original captured videos at 4K, while the CS-8 Mini did 2.7K. The CS-8 Micro is limited to 720p, but carries forward features like an 8x digital zoom and a selection of five filters that include vintage effects that add flicker and grain to emulate the look of 8mm film. Camp Snap estimates the Micro’s 4GB microSD card can hold 45 to 6o minutes of video, while its battery life is estimated to be 30 to 45 minutes on a full charge.

Correction, November 13th: The Camp Snap CS-8 Mini will launch on November 17th.

#265. Study finds slightly higher risk of autism diagnosis in areas with more lithium in drinking water, but experts say more research is needed

A new study found a moderately higher risk of autism spectrum disorder in children born to pregnant people exposed to tap water with higher levels of lithium, but experts caution that this association does not show a direct link between the two.

About 1 in 36 children in the US is diagnosed with autism spectrum disorder (ASD) each year, according to data from the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

Scientists still don’t know the exact cause of autism, a developmental disorder. Genetics may be a factor, but some have been looking at potential environmental causes, too.

Autism diagnosis has become more common, but pandemic disrupted early detection, CDC says

Cases may be on the rise, but that is also unclear. One study published this year on cases in the New York-New Jersey area found that autism diagnosis rates tripled among certain age groups between 2000 and 2016. A 2021 report found similar increases in cases, but the CDC says the increased number of cases is most likely linked to more doctors screening for the condition.

Lithium is an alkali metal that can be found naturally in some food and ground water. It’s used in batteries, grease and air conditioners, as well as in the treatment of bipolar disorder and some blood disorders. Its levels in US drinking water are not regulated, according to the US Geological Survey.

A new study, published Monday in the journal JAMA Pediatrics, found a small association between lithium and autism diagnosis in Denmark, where the researchers say the level of lithium in drinking water is similar to that in American water systems.

The researchers checked a database of people with psychiatric disorders for children born between 2000 and 2013 to find information on 8,842 cases of ASD and 43,864 participants who did not have ASD. They then measured the concentration of lithium in 151 public waterworks that served more than half of the Danish population and mapped out where pregnant people lived in relation.

For many neurodiverse people, 'The Sims' has been a lifelong comfort

As lithium levels in water increased, there was a modest increased risk of an ASD diagnosis. Specifically, compared with people at the lowest exposure level, those who had the second and third highest exposure during pregnancy had a 24% to 26% higher risk of ASD diagnosed in children. The group with the highest exposure had a 46% higher risk than those at the lowest level of exposure.

The researchers could not tell how much water the pregnant people drank, but they picked Denmark in part because residents there consume some of the lowest amounts of bottled water in Europe.

Experts say it’s important to note that the research can’t show that lithium exposure leads directly to an autism diagnosis.

Further study is required, said study co-author Dr. Beate Ritz, a professor of neurology in the David Geffen School of Medicine at UCLA, and a professor of epidemiology and environmental health at the UCLA Fielding School of Public Health.

“Any drinking water contaminants that may affect the developing human brain deserve intense scrutiny,” Ritz said in a news release. She added that the research would need to be replicated in other countries to look for a similar connection.

The implications of the findings are complex as far as public health policy is concerned, according to an editorial published alongside the study. Lithium levels in water, at concentrations that the study associated with a potential ASD risk, have also been linked with health benefits such as lower rates of hospitalization for psychiatric disorders and suicide.

“If all these of associations are valid, the wisdom of Solomon will be required to develop guidelines for lithium in drinking water that are maximally protective of the entire population,” wrote Dr. David C. Bellinger, a professor of neurology and psychology at Harvard Medical School. “Until the basic biology of ASD is better understood, it will be difficult to distinguish causal from spurious associations.”

'Heartbreaking' delays in autism diagnosis and treatment got even worse during pandemic

Dr. Max Wiznitzer, director of the Rainbow Autism Center at University Hospitals Rainbow Babies and Children’s Hospital in Cleveland, points to other research on the effects of lithium on pregnant people who take it for mental health disorders. Those studies – which look at people exposed to much higher levels than are found in drinking water – do not show a connection with autism spectrum disorder.

“It’s an interesting association, but causation is definitely not proven,” said Wiznitzer, who was not involved in the new research. “We have to see if there’s a viable and biologically plausible mechanism by which a small amount of lithium in the water supply can somehow do this, yet pharmacologic dosing of lithium in women with bipolar disorder has not been reported to be causing increased risk of ASD.”

Other studies have also suggested connections between ASD and environmental exposures to things like pesticides, air pollution and phthalates. But none of them points to any of these factors as a direct cause of the disorder.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

A link between environmental exposure and ASD is hard to prove, Wiznitzer said. With research showing that increased exposure to air pollution raises the risk of giving birth to a child with ASD, for example, he often wonders whether pollution is the determining factor or if it’s just the populations who live in more polluted areas.

“There’s a lot of speculation about about environmental factors, but how many of them are truly causally associated?” Wiznitzer said. “We are bombarded with a variety of environmental stressors in our everyday lives. We have to figure out how to basically safely navigate them, and this is probably not one that’s high on our list.”
