#263. Jensen Huang was reckless with the environment, too

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Nvidia’s environmental footprint extends way beyond its own offices.

Nvidia’s environmental footprint extends way beyond its own offices.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Under cofounder Jensen Huang, Nvidia has become the engine for the AI boom — and one of the tech industry’s biggest environmental liabilities. Its sought-after chips have triggered a new wave of water- and chemical-intensive factories, drained power grids from inside massive data centers, and set the stage for a looming e-waste problem.

Under Huang’s leadership, Nvidia clashed with environmental groups seeking to hold the company accountable for its so-called “hidden” emissions. Huang has talked up the idea of using AI to fight climate change while his company’s supply chain emissions ballooned. Nvidia’s investments, meanwhile, have supported the buildout of data centers that have sparked intense backlash for both their local and global impact. Now, after the CEO’s shocking resignation amid a sprawling sex-and-espionage scandal, advocates are urging Nvidia to turn the page.

He compared AI to a surgeon who has “to cut you open to save you.”

“Jensen Huang has failed to put climate responsibility at the heart of the company’s growth strategy, to slow down the unchecked expansion of data centres or to prevent its chips from being deployed in unethical applications like automated warfare,” Avex Li, Greenpeace East Asia’s project lead for AI supply chain campaign, said in a press release Thursday. The organization is now pushing interim CEO Colette Kress to commit Nvidia to decarbonizing by investing in renewable energy, as well as ensuring its technology is used in a way that “respects human rights.” Nvidia didn’t immediately respond to requests for comment from The Verge.

Huang has projected optimism about how AI can boost the adoption of renewable energy, even as the planet-heating emissions of tech companies — including his own — have grown in the generative AI era. “If you want to turn a corner on climate change, if you want a future that’s sustainable, lean into A.I. It is the best opportunity we have to get there,” Huang said in a September episode of The Ezra Klein Show, arguing that electricity demand for AI is leading companies to buy more sustainable energy.

In the same interview, however, he admitted data centers will rely on more fossil fuels in the near term, and that building up the infrastructure for AI will likely result in “an enormous amount of pain and suffering.” He compared AI to a surgeon who has “to cut you open to save you.”

Nvidia’s supply-chain emissions have grown 725 percent since 2020, according to an analysis of the company’s public disclosures by research analyst Ketan Joshi and several environmental organizations, including Greenpeace and Climate Action Against Disinformation, published in September. It’s an increase of about 9.4 million metric tons of planet-heating pollution, roughly equivalent to the emissions from nearly 25 gas plants over a year. Supply chain emissions the company disclosed for fiscal year 2026 reached around 10.7 million metric tons of CO2, according to Nvidia’s latest sustainability report.

Joshi and his co-authors approximated how much greenhouse gas pollution comes from the use of chips the company sells, a category Nvidia doesn’t include in its sustainability reports. Those emissions could have reached 21 million metric tons in 2025, according to the report, based on estimated chip power consumption. That level of pollution puts Nvidia on par with Russia’s state-owned coal company, according to Joshi.

Those “indirect” emissions — from its supply chain and the use of its products — make up the bulk of Nvidia’s climate impact. The carbon footprint of facilities Nvidia actually owns and operates looks much smaller in comparison because Nvidia designs chips that other companies like TSMC then manufacture. Greenhouse gas emissions coming directly from Nvidia-operated facilities and vehicles only reached 9,822 metric tons in fiscal year 2026, the company’s sustainability report shows. And in 2025, the company reached a goal of matching 100 percent of its facilities’ electricity use with clean energy purchases.

Greenpeace wants Nvidia to extend that goal to its entire manufacturing supply chain. When Greenpeace activists disrupted Huang’s speech at Nvidia GTC in Berlin last month, he responded onstage by saying, “I’ll talk to you guys later … All important issues, but let me get through my keynote.” In the Thursday press release, Greenpeace says Nvidia has yet to meet with Greenpeace despite the organization’s attempts to “seek dialogue” with the company over the past two years.

“One of Huang’s defining legacies is inflating a speculative AI bubble, pouring billions of dollars into infrastructure with little regard for the real-world consequences inflicted on local communities and the climate,” Katrin Wu, Big Tech campaigner at Greenpeace International, said in an email to The Verge. “Huang may be gone, but the ‘tech colonialism’ Nvidia perpetuates continues.”

“Huang may be gone, but the ‘tech colonialism’ Nvidia perpetuates continues.”

Beyond Nvidia’s day-to-day operations, Huang threw his weight around as the CEO of one of the world’s most valuable companies — chumming it up with President Donald Trump and lobbying for the US to allow sales of its powerful chips to China. The company’s influence on the global economy extended beyond chips as it invested in data centers stocking its GPUs.

And with electricity supply often the thorniest bottleneck to building more and bigger data centers, Nvidia’s long tendrils reached into the energy sector as well. NVentures, the company’s venture capital arm, has funded battery recycling company Redwood Materials and startups developing AI tools to optimize power grids and make data centers more responsive to fluctuations in energy demand.

Nvidia joined one of those startups, Emerald AI, along with Google to cofound the AI Energy Management Alliance (AEMA) in September. The alliance advocates for strategies that tech companies promote as ways to keep data centers from overwhelming power grids or raising utility bills for other customers, including generating power on-site and making “flexible” facilities that can pause operations during times of peak energy demand. Notably, some environmental groups argue that those measures might just help data centers come online faster without fully insulating households from the impact that rising electricity demand has on their air quality or utility bills.

Whether Nvidia’s environmental footprint shrinks or expands from here will depend on whoever steps in to fill Huang’s shoes. Over the years, Greenpeace and other environmental groups have pointed out that despite having an outsize impact on the environment, Nvidia doesn’t necessarily come under the same public scrutiny as more consumer-facing brands like Apple or Google. Huang’s exploits have thrust the company into a new kind of spotlight, however, just as data centers increasingly face backlash from nearby communities. Nvidia won’t be able to hide from the glare.

#262. Pope Leo XIV Goes Home to Peru, Meeting Ghosts of His Past

In Peru, where Leo XIV lived for two decades and became a citizen, the pope was met by President Keiko Fujimori, whose father’s policies he had protested.

Motoko Rich, the Rome bureau chief, is traveling with Pope Leo XIV during his tour of Uruguay, Argentina and Peru. Mitra Taj reported from Lima, Peru.

Just a year and a half into his papacy and less than a month after surgeons removed a lobe of his right lung, Pope Leo XIV, the first pontiff born in the United States, touched down in his homeland on Wednesday.

Yet instead of setting foot on American soil, he arrived in Peru, where he spent two decades as a missionary, priest and bishop and became a citizen in 2015.

“Today I understand how much of this land remains with me and how much of my own story also belongs to you,” Leo said, speaking on Wednesday evening to Peru’s president, civic leaders and diplomats at the Gran Teatro Nacional, a theater and concert hall in the country’s capital, Lima. One of his biggest applause lines was when he referred to Peru as “my homeland.”

Having turned out record crowds at Masses in Argentina, the pope flew from Buenos Aires to Lima on Wednesday afternoon, reaching the final country of his 12-day tour of South America. The pope emerged at the top of the stairs next to a sign on the side of his plane that read “Pontifex Maximus,” and was greeted by Peru’s president, Keiko Fujimori, and a chorus of singing children on a red carpet at the Callao Air Base near Lima.

As his motorcade made its way through city streets under gray skies, about 3 million people lined the roads, according to local authorities, holding up cellphones as they waited for a glimpse of the pontiff.

His choice to visit Peru so soon after becoming pope — while deferring a visit to the country of his birth — underscored the deep emotional ties he feels to the place that formed him as a priest.

#261. Starship flight 15 powers to orbit—setting up a possible catch on Saturday

One way or another, the company will get some more data on Saturday.

STARBASE, Texas—Starship lifted off right on schedule Friday from South Texas, making its 15th test flight in more than three years.

The largely successful launch took place in the early evening; as the massive rocket rose into the atmosphere, the Sun sank toward the western horizon. Conditions were ideal for the launch, with the atmospheric winds so calm the rocket’s white plume did not dissipate for many minutes. The weather in South Texas is not always comfortable; summers are long and hot. Friday evening was perfect at Starbase.

The first stage ascended toward space flawlessly, releasing the upper stage above the Gulf of Mexico. From there, the Starship upper stage ignited its Raptor engines and powered its way into a nominal orbit. This was the second time SpaceX has flown Starship into low-Earth orbit.

When it came time for the first stage to return to the ground, it dropped like a rock. However, due to an unspecified issue, the vehicle was commanded to divert into the Gulf rather than return to the Starbase facility to be caught by the launch tower. It created a fantastically powerful explosion, even for observers more than 10 miles away.

The primary aim of Friday’s launch was the deployment of 26 Starlink V3 satellites from a narrow door at the base of the spacecraft. This occurred about 40 minutes into Starship’s flight, and all 26 satellites—which have solar panels that stretch 60 meters end to end when fully deployed—safely made it into orbit, where they will now use onboard propulsion to raise their altitude.

This means that Starship is now an operational, orbital rocket. It is the largest and most powerful machine humans have ever launched into space. The challenge for the company now is to make Starship reusable and dramatically increase its flight rate.

In that sense, the first part of Friday’s mission was not a success. SpaceX had hoped to return the first-stage booster, refurbish it, and fly it again. The company has previously landed and reflown Starship boosters but has yet to do so with the latest iteration of the vehicle, version 3.

However, SpaceX still has a chance to make history on Saturday with the potential return of the Starship upper stage. SpaceX has never attempted to catch the vehicle’s upper stage.

Doing so will require Starship to pass a series of tests in orbit and subsequently during its return to Earth to ensure it is fully healthy. This is critical because Starship will be passing over parts of Mexico and the United States before returning to Starbase, and SpaceX does not want to endanger people on the ground. Additionally, it does not want to risk damage to its launch towers.

If Starship passes these health checks, it would land at about 3:15 pm local time in Texas (21:15 UTC). If it does not, the company has earlier options to de-orbit the vehicle into an ocean.

Successfully returning Starship’s upper stage would be historic, as well as significant for SpaceX’s efforts to reuse the massive rocket. It would also give the company valuable information about the vehicle’s performance during reentry.

One of the biggest hurdles left in Starship’s development is the vehicle’s heat shield, which must protect the upper stage during its return through Earth’s atmosphere. More than that, SpaceX aims to make Starship rapidly reusable, and to do this, Starship’s tiles must be light and resilient.

SpaceX has been experimenting with tiles since the beginning of the Starship program, trying to find the right combination of low mass, heat resistance, and durability so the tiles require minimal refurbishment between launches. For this flight, SpaceX said it is applying learnings from the previous test flight in September, including additional retention mechanisms on tiles in areas deemed to be at the highest risk of falling off during ascent and addressing areas with a flow path for plasma to get behind the tiles.

Another problem on recent flights has been heat incursion into the small gaps between the edges of the tiles. According to SpaceX, this Starship has a large portion of the heat shield equipped with a curved tile design that has shown the ability to reduce heating in the gaps between tiles.

#260. Syrian refugee elected mayor of German town, years after fleeing war

A Syrian who arrived in Germany as a refugee in 2015 has won a mayoral election in the southwestern German state of Baden-Württemberg.

Ryyan Alshebl, who left his hometown of As Suwayda in Syria eight years ago, ran as an independent in the municipality of Ostelsheim. He won 55.41% of the votes on Sunday, beating two German candidates, Marco Strauss and Mathias Fey.

Locals cheered the 29-year-old when he welcomed his win, a victory he described as “sensational,” German local broadcaster SWR reported Monday.

“Today, Ostelsheim sent an example for broad-mindedness and cosmopolitanism for the whole of Germany,” he said, according to to German public broadcaster ZDF. “That’s not something that can be taken for granted in a conservative, rural area.”

Alshebl’s first call after his victory was to his mother in Syria, who was thrilled with the news, SWR reported.

The Association of Municipalities of Baden-Württemberg said Alshebl is the first man with Syrian roots to run for and win a mayor’s office. He will start his role in June.

'You are not a refugee.' Roma refugees fleeing war in Ukraine say they are suffering discrimination and prejudice

Ostelsheim residents have welcomed their incoming mayor. “The fairy tale has come true, and the right man has become our mayor,” Annette Keck, who lives in the village, told SWR.

Strauss, one of his opponents, congratulated Alshebl. “I wish you good luck and at the same time ask for support for Mr. Alshebl, for our shared Ostelsheim,” he said on Facebook.

The state’s Integration Minister Manne Lucha said that Alshebl’s victory showed that diversity is a natural part of Baden-Württemberg. “I would be very pleased if Ryyan Alshebl’s election encourages more people with a migration history to run for political office,” he said.

Not everyone has been so warm to the 29-year-old. ZDF reported the Syrian received hateful comments on the campaign trail.

The young politician went from house to house, promoting his election program, and “the experiences were predominantly positive,” but there was also a minority of far-right fringe voters in Ostelsheim that did not want to accept him due to his Syrian roots, Alshebl told ZDF.

Born to a schoolteacher and agricultural engineer in Syria, Alshebl described his life as carefree until the age of 20, according to his campaign website.

At the time, protests against the Syrian government that began in 2011 soon devolved into chaotic war. The fighting and later rise of ISIS forced 10.6 million people from home by late 2015 – about half of Syria’s pre-war population.

Alshebl faced the dilemma of being drafted for military service with the Syrian army or leaving the country, according to his website.

While many Syrians were displaced internally or fled to countries in the region, others like Alshebl made the dangerous journey to Europe. He was 21 years old at the time, and said he crossed from Turkey to the Greek island of Lesbos in a rubber dinghy.

Former German Chancellor Angela Merkel had implemented a brief open-door policy in 2015 that saw the country take in about 1.2 million asylum seekers in the following years, including Alshebl.

They were welcomed into British homes. Celebrating their first Christmas together, Ukrainians wonder if that hospitality will last

The move sparked a backlash in Germany and the sudden growth of the far-right, anti-immigration Alternative for Germany (AfD) in the wake of summer 2015.

Once in Germany, Alshebl lived close to Ostelsheim and said at the time he felt “there is only one thing you can do: get back on your feet quickly and start investing in your own future quickly.”

For the last seven years he worked in the administration of Althengstett town hall, in a neighboring town. He drew from his experience, he said in his campaign, and made digital access to to public administration services one of priorities. Flexible childcare and climate protections are also on his agenda.

Alshebl, who is a member of the Green Party and now has German citizenship, pledged during his campaign that once elected as mayor he would move to Ostelsheim.

#258. You can adjust the position of the trackball on Keychron’s new wireless mouse

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A pair of dials underneath the T1 HE let you fine-tune where its trackball is positioned under your thumb.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Nape Pro was Keychron’s first trackball device that was introduced at CES 2026 with a slim and compact design allowing it to be positioned right up against a keyboard. The company’s new T1 HE instead looks more like other trackball mice already available from companies like Logitech and Kensington with a 34mm ball positioned on the side to improve ergonomics. But since hands and fingers come in various sizes, the T1 HE allows the position of its trackball to be fine-tuned with minor positional adjustments so it rests squarely under your thumb without any reaching.

The T1 HE is now available for preorder through Keychron’s online store for $59.99 in black, white, or translucent color options. Shipping is expected to start in early December. A pair of dials on the underside of the mouse are used to adjust the vertical and horizontal position of the trackball, which can also be extended or retracted. The adjustments are limited to just a few millimeters in either direction, but those minor improvements to ergonomics could go a long way to making the T1 HE more comfortable to use on a regular basis.

The sensitivity of the T1 HE’s optical sensor can be adjusted from 100 DPI to 2,400 DPI using a button that cycles through presets or the Keychron Launcher software that also allows for other customizations including button remapping and creating macros. The mouse features six customizable buttons in total, and a 600mAh battery good for up to 106 hours of use in Bluetooth mode, or 98 hours when using an included 2.4GHz wireless receiver. If you don’t want to worry about charging, the T1 HE Wireless Adjustable Trackball Mouse can also be used with a USB-C cable.

The Israeli authorities said that Hamas had established a command center at the hospital complex. Hamas said the attack was “a heinous crime targeting a civilian medical facility.”

The Israeli military struck a hospital in the southern Gaza Strip on Thursday, killing at least six people and injuring 28 more, according to local health officials.

Dr. Ahmed al-Farra, general director of Nasser Hospital, said in a news conference on Thursday that two missiles had hit the facility and that among the dead was Rezeq Abu Shaira, 52, a doctor.

#256. Winemakers are learning that conservation can be good for business

Healthy soils, native plants, and fewer chemicals are helping vineyards adapt.

More than 500 vineyards are scattered across the slopes and valleys of South Africa’s Western Cape, one of the world’s most biologically diverse landscapes. The region produces about 90 percent of South Africa’s wine, but winemakers there are increasingly struggling with drought, excessive heat and erratic rainfall.

Temperatures in the region have risen 1.5 to 2 times faster than the global average over the past five decades. And extreme storms have become more intense as climate variability increases. The late 2010s brought a significant challenge for winemakers, as three years of severe drought placed vineyards under intense stress and reduced grape production.

As climate variability places increasing pressure on grape production, many wine producers are building on sustainability measures that have long been part of vineyard management.

We are a team of geographers and sustainability scientists who study environmental change, environmental health, conservation, agriculture and land management. Our team wanted to better understand how farmers in South Africa’s wine-producing regions are responding as a range of environmental and economic pressures rise. We talked with growers, conservation practitioners and wine industry experts in the Western Cape and analyzed how 107 vineyards, about 20% of the total, were using sustainable land stewardship practices to adapt.

In many agricultural landscapes, production and conservation goals are frequently viewed as competing priorities. But our research found that many vineyard operators in the Western Cape see long-term stewardship and conservation of soil and wildlife habitat as essential strategies for business success.

The Western Cape is home to the Cape Floral Region, a global biodiversity hot spot. It includes fynbos, a unique biome containing thousands of endemic plant species, including types of proteas, ericas and restios that are found nowhere else on Earth.

Sustainability certification and conservation programs, such as World Wildlife Fund Conservation Champions, have helped encourage vineyards to protect biodiversity in the region. Many growers use these sustainability certifications to increase their wine’s marketability. But we found they have also embraced conservation for how it can benefit their vineyards. Rather than viewing conservation as land taken out of production, many said they see it supporting the ecological processes essential to their business.

The majority of South Africa’s vineyards today participate in the Integrated Production of Wine certification system, created by the South African wine industry and researchers in 1998 to reduce environmental harm. The system sets standards governing pesticide use, water management, wastewater treatment and environmental stewardship. It also uses independent auditing of participating farms and wineries to ensure those claiming certification meet the standards.

To manage the water supplies their vineyards depend on, growers described restoring native vegetation, including the removal of alien species, along waterways and reducing erosion to protect wetlands and rivers. Research shows that healthy natural systems can improve water retention in the soil and contribute to the overall resilience of the landscape in the face of more extreme climate conditions.

To boost their soil’s health, some growers are planting cover crops, such as legumes, cereals like oats and rye, and brassicas such as radish and mustard, between vine rows. Growers broadly described using cover crops, compost, mulching and other practices to improve soil structure and increase organic matter in the soil. Cover crops require maintenance and can provide habitat for pests, but research shows that the right cover crops, when well maintained, can benefit vineyards by reducing erosion and increasing water retention and nutrients in the soil.

Several producers also described efforts to reduce their reliance on chemical pesticides, which is beneficial not only for the environment but also their businesses as chemical costs continue to rise. We heard about vineyards that monitor pest populations closely and respond in targeted ways rather than using pesticides broadly.

Some growers encourage beneficial insects and manage habitats that support natural pest control. The creation of habitats for natural pest predators can reduce the overall disease risk too. While chemical treatments remain part of many vineyard operations, growers reported increasing interest in other approaches that prevent disease and pest outbreaks, such as pruning, to reduce their total chemical use.

Sustainable practices like these are well established in South Africa’s wine industry, but our interviews suggest their use has been expanding in recent years. Rather than focusing solely on treating problems after they arise, these approaches emphasize prevention, monitoring and ecological balance.

The stewardship practices we observed in South Africa are investments in long-term business resilience for many growers.

Many of the wine producers we interviewed described facing rising costs and market uncertainty. Exports, labor and chemicals are all expensive. They emphasized that maintaining healthy soils, conserving water and reducing vulnerability to drought and disease are essential to keeping their vineyards productive.

As concerns about drought, climate pressures and profitability rise, the area in vineyard production in South Africa has also decreased. Several growers described diversifying their business models and even switching crops to make their businesses more resilient to hotter, drier conditions.

Farmers around the world are confronting the realities of a changing climate. Our research shows that effective solutions can come from recognizing the interconnectedness of nature and production.

The experience of South Africa’s winegrowers shows that farms can be managed more sustainably when they’re seen as part of larger ecological systems.

Students Zoe Van der Walt, Jack Spining and Dryver Finch contributed to this article.

Hannah V. Herrero is Associate Professor of Geography & Sustainability and Director of Sustainability at University of Tennessee; Erin L. Bunting is Assistant Professor of Geography at Michigan State University; Jane Southworth is Professor of Geography at University of Florida; Jason K. Blackburn is Professor of Geography at University of Florida; and Stephanie Insalaco-Wyner is Assistant Professor of Geographic Information Sciences at Southwestern University.

#254. These are the animals people most want to see photographed

Editor’s Note:  Call to Earth is a CNN editorial series committed to reporting on the environmental challenges facing our planet, together with the solutions. Rolex’s Perpetual Planet initiative has partnered with CNN to drive awareness and education around key sustainability issues and to inspire positive action.

Originally a term used by trophy hunters in Africa, the “Big Five” described the animals most challenging to shoot and kill: the lion, elephant, leopard, rhino, and buffalo. Nowadays, it is more loosely used to refer to some of Africa’s largest and most iconic animals.

However, British photographer Graeme Green has reclaimed the narrative, creating a global “New Big Five” for wildlife photography. In 2021, 50,000 people around the world voted for the five animals they would most like to photograph, or see photographed, in the wild. Five animals were crowned the winners: the elephant, polar bear, lion, gorilla and tiger.

This week, sees the publication of “The New Big 5” photography book, which features images of those animals and other at-risk wildlife, from photography legends such as Ami Vitale, Steve McCurry and Paul Nicklen, and essays from famous conservationists and activists including like Jane Goodall and Paula Kahumbu.

Green says that the book celebrates wildlife and is a global call to action on issues impacting wildlife, including habitat loss, poaching, pollution and climate change.

Green was on assignment in Botswana at least a decade ago when he came up with the idea for a project to encourage people to “shoot with a camera, not a gun,” he says.

“I thought this would be a way to get people really focusing on wildlife, thinking about the wildlife they love, thinking about the animals that are at risk.”

Ten photographs that made the world wake up to climate change

In total, the book includes the work of 144 globally renowned wildlife photographers from Ecuador to India. Curating the images took nearly two years of work, says Green.

“I think these are some of the most beautiful and creative images that I’ve seen put together in one book,” says Green. “These are the species that we are at risk of losing.”

According to the UN, nature is declining at an unprecedented rate, with around one million of the planet’s animal and plant species facing extinction. The “New Big 5,” all of which are threatened, act as ambassadors for what is happening in the natural world, says Green.

As well as being a powerful reminder of what we stand to lose, the book also points people towards potential solutions. Featured essays explore the benefits of rewilding and the importance of indigenous communities in conservation.

A chapter on endangered species from bees to blue whales illustrates the alarming threat climate change poses to animals outside of the “New Big 5.” “That’s only the tip of the iceberg – I could have included thousands of pictures because that’s how serious the situation is,” says Green.

Why a wildlife photographer took 40,000 photos to capture these extraordinary birds

Jane Goodall, a leading conservationist who also wrote the afterword to the book, said in a press release that “we have a window of time during which we can start to heal some of the harm we have inflicted on the natural world, but only if we get together and take action now.

“I hope the photos will lead people into the wonderful worlds of these iconic species. Then, perhaps, other people will become involved in helping to create a world where wildlife can flourish for future generations to enjoy,” she said.

“The New Big 5: A Global Photography Project For Endangered Wildlife,” by Graeme Green, published by Earth Aware Editions, is on sale from April 4, 2023.

This story has been updated to reflect the range of issues covered by the book.

#251. James Webb telescope finds ice older than the star it orbits

Radiation and collisions reshape the planetary building materials.

Beta Pictoris is a young star, roughly 20 million years old, and located about 64 light-years away from Earth. It is surrounded by a vast disk of dust and rubble where planets are still forming—astronomers have already found three. In a new Science study, a team led by Sarah Betti, an astronomer at the Space Telescope Science Institute in Baltimore, used the James Webb Space Telescope to look for ice in this disk. Researchers found both water and carbon dioxide ice.

The water ice is glassy rather than crystalline, and it is mixed with carbon dioxide in roughly the same proportions we see in comets of our own Solar System. This suggests ingredients necessary to form water-rich worlds might be common around young stars.

Beta Pictoris is one of the best studied stars in the sky. Its disk was among the first ever imaged, back in 1984, and we see it almost exactly edge-on, which makes it a natural laboratory for studying how planetary systems grow. It’s what astronomers call a debris disk, where the dust is constantly replenished by collisions among comets and planetesimals, small bodies that are the building blocks of planets.

The outer parts of the disk are far enough from the star that water and other volatile molecules should freeze into solid ice. The disk holds a large reservoir of carbon monoxide gas, which ultraviolet light from the star should destroy within 50 to 300 years. For that gas to still be there, something must keep replenishing it—most likely it’s released from ice when volatile-rich comets collide. The properties of the planet Beta Pictoris b also suggest it formed from ice-rich material.

Yet despite plenty of reasons to suspect the system is full of ice, earlier searches have failed to find it. Attempts to detect water vapor found nothing, and a search for the telltale dip that water ice leaves in starlight at a wavelength of 3 micrometers also failed.

According to theoretical models, ultraviolet radiation from the star can destroy small grains of pure ice faster than collisions can produce them. On the other hand, grains of dirty ice, mixed with other molecules, should survive longer.

Betti and her colleagues observed Beta Pictoris on December 7, 2023, using Webb’s Near Infrared Spectrograph (NIRSpec). They operated it in a mode that records a full spectrum, from 0.6 to 5.2 micrometers, for every point in the image. After subtracting the overwhelming glare of the star, the team measured how much starlight the disk’s dust reflects at each wavelength to look for the fingerprints of ices.

Water ice absorbs light strongly near 3 micrometers, while carbon dioxide ice absorbs either at 4.27 micrometers or 4.39 micrometers when it contains the heavier carbon-13 isotope. The team first mapped the water ice features across the disk and found they were strikingly uniform. They then extracted detailed spectra from two spots on opposite sides of the star, each roughly 100 astronomical units out.

Both spectra showed water and carbon dioxide ices, the latter in both its common form and the rarer carbon-13 version. But the shape of those features turned out to tell us more than the fact they were there.

The carbon dioxide features were shifted slightly toward shorter wavelengths and were much broader than those of pure carbon dioxide ice measured in the lab. The team says in the paper that this is how carbon dioxide behaves when its molecules are trapped inside water ice. In other words, the two ices aren’t sitting side by side as separate grains—they are thoroughly mixed, which makes it the kind of dirty ice that models suggested could survive the star’s ultraviolet radiation.

The team calculated that there is slightly more than one carbon dioxide molecule for every 10 water molecules, meaning it’s about 11 percent of the ice. That falls within the 2.5 to 30 percent range measured in Solar System comets and the 7 to 20 percent seen in the ice of disks around other newborn stars. It means the ices of Beta Pictoris may have formed through the same processes as in our own Solar System, building up on dust grains in cold, dense, water-rich clouds during the earliest stages of star formation.

The structure of the water ice also pointed in this direction. When water freezes at moderate temperatures, its molecules settle into an orderly crystal lattice, and crystalline ice produces a sharp peak in the spectrum just above 3 micrometers. That peak was absent at Beta Pictoris. The ice is almost entirely amorphous, a disordered, glass-like solid that forms when water molecules freeze so fast and so cold that they stick wherever they land. The team estimates that less than 7 percent of the water ice in the system is crystalline, which is somewhat surprising.

Models predicted that ice in debris disks should mostly be crystalline. For amorphous ice to stay in that form, it has to remain colder than about 100 Kelvin, or minus 173° Celsius. Otherwise, something has to keep breaking up its crystal structure, such as high-energy ultraviolet light, cosmic rays, or particles streaming from the star. Either way, the researchers concluded the ice most likely formed in the interstellar medium, long before the planets of Beta Pictoris began to take shape.

The remaining question was how the icy grains got spread so evenly across the disk.

The team argues that this even ice distribution requires frequent collisions among planetesimals. Each collision would release fresh icy dust, including ice that had been buried and protected deep inside the colliding bodies, replenishing what the starlight destroys. We already have evidence that such collisions do happen. Earlier infrared images revealed a curved, lopsided streak of dust in the southwestern arm of the disk, thought to be debris from a violent collision a little less than halfway out from the star.

Betti’s team’s findings support the idea that the disk’s carbon monoxide gas comes from colliding icy bodies and offer an explanation of how ice can survive in an environment constantly exposed to ultraviolet light. What the team could not do is determine exactly how much crystalline ice is present or rule out other molecules hiding in the mixture.

But if a young planetary system 64 light-years away may have started with much the same icy ingredients as ours, then primordial, interstellar ice might be a common ingredient of planetary systems in general. This means the raw materials for water-rich, potentially habitable worlds might be common, too.

Science, 2026. DOI: 10.1126/science.adw7770

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

#250. Tourists Brave a Muddy Jungle Trek to See a Famed World War II Wreck

Seven Chinese tourists, a dozen villagers and a couple of journalists forded streams and bushwhacked to the downed plane of Isoroku Yamamoto, the Japanese admiral. Machetes proved handy.

Seven Chinese tourists, a dozen villagers and a couple of journalists forded streams and bushwhacked to the downed plane of Isoroku Yamamoto, the Japanese admiral. Machetes proved handy.

Wading through streams and bushes to the plane wreckage, where a tourist posed for pictures.Credit...

Reporting from Bougainville, Papua New Guinea

Halfway through the morning, I’m being tossed around like a popcorn kernel in the back of a troop carrier playing an eclectic medley of Backstreet Boys and Papua New Guinean jams.

We’re traveling along what feels more like a collection of loose rocks, arranged to be roughly vehicle width, than a road. I’ve lost count of the number of river crossings, including one where the water lapped a few inches from the car’s windows. And this is during a dry spell.

Our caravan is headed for an unlikely tourist destination deep in the jungle on Bougainville Island, an autonomous region of Papua New Guinea in the Solomon Sea.

The piecemeal remnants of a Mitsubishi G4M1 — Betty, as the planes were code-named by the Allies in World War II — have remained for some 80 years at the crash site of Japanese Navy Adm. Isoroku Yamamoto. This is the spot where Admiral Yamamoto, mastermind of the Pearl Harbor attack, archvillain to Americans in the war, a respected and memorialized figure to the Japanese, met his end in 1943.

Bougainville was occupied by the Japanese in early 1942, in the months after Pearl Harbor, as the Imperial Navy pushed into the South Pacific, with Admiral Yamamoto at the helm. In April 1943, Allied forces intercepted and deciphered Japanese communication on the admiral’s travels, and American P-38 Lightning fighters gunned down his plane over southern Bougainville, a feat the Americans triumphantly dubbed Operation Vengeance.

#249. ChatGPT Wallet lets OpenAI’s Dots agents make purchases for you

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

OpenAI says ChatGPT Wallet won’t share your actual credit card info with its AI agents.﻿

OpenAI says ChatGPT Wallet won’t share your actual credit card info with its AI agents.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After adding a card to the wallet, your Dots agents can use it to buy things in response to your prompts, but they’ll check in with you each time before checking out. OpenAI’s support page says every purchase with ChatGPT Wallet requires a security check with biometric verification like Face ID or a fingerprint scan.

After you review the AI agent’s purchase and sign off on it, OpenAI says that, similar to using tap to pay, Apple Pay, or Google Wallet, it substitutes your credit card details with a payment token, so your actual card info isn’t shared with your Dots agent or the business you’re buying from. Eventually, OpenAI says it expects “users to be able to delegate purchases entirely to their agent by setting clear parameters—such as spending limits, purchase categories, or time periods—and pre-approve those purchases.”

ChatGPT Wallet might not work everywhere. Some retailers have restrictions on AI agents or block them altogether. Earlier this year, Amazon blocked Meta’s Muse agents. OpenAI says that in order for agents to use ChatGPT Wallet, “websites need to ensure agents are not inadvertently blocked, and payment and risk systems need to recognize legitimate purchases made by agents.”

#247. Giving Marjorie Taylor Greene a platform isn't good for America

Editor’s Note:  Dean Obeidallah, a former attorney, is the host of SiriusXM radio’s daily program “The Dean Obeidallah Show.” Follow him @DeanObeidallah@masto.ai. The opinions expressed in this commentary are his own. View more opinion on CNN.

Last year, GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene of Georgia spoke at a white nationalist event organized by Holocaust denier Nick Fuentes that caused Republican leaders to denounce her.

Last week, Greene’s Twitter account was temporarily suspended by the Elon Musk-headed platform over a tweet with a graphic referring to a “Trans Day of Vengeance,” as she denounced a planned transgender rights rally.

And come Tuesday, Greene has announced plans to protest in New York City when former President Donald Trump is expected to be arraigned on an indictment of more than 30 counts, calling the proceedings against him an “unconstitutional WITCH HUNT!”

But on Sunday, Greene was featured on CBS’ “60 Minutes” in an interview the long-running show promoted on Twitter with the tease: “Republican Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene, nicknamed MTG, isn’t afraid to share her opinions, no matter how intense and in-your-face they are. She sits down with Lesley Stahl this Sunday on 60 Minutes.” The images attached to this tweet by “60 Minutes” include Greene and Stahl walking through the US Capitol, taking a stroll outside and Greene showing Stahl something on her phone.

It's time for Marjorie Taylor Greene to go

In the segment that aired Sunday night, Stahl noted the congresswoman had moved from the fringe to the GOP’s front row in two years despite a “sharp tongue” and “some pretty radical views” as well as “over the top” comments such as “the Democrats are a party of pedophiles.” Stahl also referred to video of Greene chasing a Parkland, Florida, school shooting survivor, still maintaining that the 2020 election was stolen and failing to criticize Trump over spending. (The interview was conducted before news of his indictment.)

But Stahl didn’t mention Greene spoke at a white nationalist event a year ago while a member of Congress or her extreme anti-Muslim views and her defense of January 6 rioters.

Criticism of CBS for amplifying Greene has been swift and well-deserved even before the program aired. Former GOP Rep. Adam Kinzinger of Illinois tweeted beforehand: “Wow. Insane that 60 min would do this.” (Kinzinger is a CNN senior political commentator.)

Journalist Molly Jong-Fast also slammed “60 Minutes” with the tweet: “Attention is currency and 60 minutes is spending its currency on the Jewish space lasers woman.” (Jong-Fast was apparently referring to Greene’s past claim that a massive California wildfire was started by “a laser” beamed from space controlled by a prominent Jewish banking family.)

David Hogg, who survived the 2018 horrific school shooting at Marjory Stoneman Douglas High School in Parkland and has since become an activist against gun violence, responded, “I look forward to your questions about why she thinks school shootings are fake and why she’s supported QAnon.”

Greene amplified the conspiracy theory — two years before being elected to Congress — that the Parkland shooting that took 17 lives was staged. And in 2019, Greene appeared on video confronting Hogg outside the Capitol when he was championing laws to save lives from gun violence, screaming that the then-teenager was a “coward.” She also called Hogg “#littleHitler” on social media.

Greene’s own tweet promoting Sunday’s segment was not filled with her typical smears of “fake news” when she doesn’t like the coverage. Rather, Greene urged people to tune in as she praised “60 Minutes” host Stahl (and misspelled her first name): “It was an honor to spend a few days with the legendary icon Leslie Stahl and talented crew @60Minutes.” Greene added, “Leslie is a trailblazer for women in journalism. And while we may disagree on some issues, I respect her greatly.”

Now, it is true that “60 Minutes” over its 50-plus-year history has featured what the show has dubbed “controversial” guests. These include a 2000 interview with Oklahoma City bomber Timothy McVeigh, whose 1995 terrorist attack left 168 dead, including 19 children. And the show did a 1979 interview with Iran’s Ayatollah Ruhollah Khomeini.

Perhaps “60 Minutes” views Greene — who has repeatedly downplayed and even defended the January 6, 2021, attack plus called for the GOP to become the party of “Christian nationalism” — in the vein of its long list of controversial guests. (CBS had not responded to a request from CNN for comment at time of publishing.)

However, the choice of Greene as a guest instantly recalls the comments of then-CBS CEO Les Moonves during Trump’s 2016 presidential campaign when he admitted that Trump’s candidacy “may not be good for America, but it’s damn good for CBS.” Moonves added, “The money’s rolling in and this is fun. … (T)his is going to be a very good year for us,” concluding, “Sorry. It’s a terrible thing to say. But bring it on, Donald. Keep going.”

Moonves may be gone from CBS, but it appears his mindset continues at “60 Minutes.” And that may be good for “60 Minutes,” but it’s definitely not good for America.

#246. Amazon has a plan to index the world’s online shopping data

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Listing the details of everything for sale everywhere is meant to make Amazon more useful than Reddit for people and AI agents.﻿

Listing the details of everything for sale everywhere is meant to make Amazon more useful than Reddit for people and AI agents.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Amazon has plans to build a vault of data on every product sold anywhere on the internet, even products Amazon doesn’t sell, and make the information available through its own site, according to Reuters. The project, called the “World Product Graph,” is meant to make it less likely that shoppers leave Amazon to do their product research on Reddit or Google, and make it more likely shoppers end up at Amazon even if they’re searching for a product sold elsewhere. The same goes for AI agents — if Amazon has the largest stockpile of shopping data, AI agents may rely on its index of products over others, so even people shopping with bots will end up on its pages.

Links to outside retailers’ products have already started popping up on Amazon through the “shop direct” feature it launched last year. The company also has its own AI shopping assistant, Alexa for Shopping, previously called Rufus. The World Product Graph project would also be aimed at fixing issues with the AI by providing it more data. According to Reuters, an internal Amazon document on the project says “Many Rufus answers are factually wrong or incomplete” and running users’ Amazon searches with the AI is expensive. Adding more data is reportedly supposed to make the system more accurate and able to use cheaper models.

Whether this actually ships is still up in the air. The internal document reportedly says the project “depends on discovering how brands organize their products on their own websites,” which could involve Amazon crawling other retailers’ sites. Reuters says it’s unclear “if or when this plan may be implemented,” but said the document on it included an October 1st, 2027 release date.

Amazon said in a statement to Reuters that “Draft, outdated documents often paint an incomplete picture and the existence of a brainstorming document such as this one doesn’t necessarily reflect our current plans.”

#245. Indonesia’s Vice President, Gibran, Resigns

Gibran Rakabuming Raka, who is stepping down amid a scandal over his academic credentials, was a symbol of the partnership that elevated President Prabowo Subianto into office.

The vice president of Indonesia submitted his resignation to Parliament on Thursday, succumbing to a scandal about his academic credentials and injecting new political uncertainty in Southeast Asia’s largest economy.

Gibran Rakabuming Raka was elected as Indonesia’s youngest vice president in 2024, in a precipitous rise. He was a symbol of the partnership between his father, the outgoing president, Joko Widodo, and the incoming leader, Prabowo Subianto, that elevated Mr. Prabowo into office.

“The relationship has clearly fractured,” said Yoes C. Kenawas, a political analyst at the Indonesian Survey Institute. With Mr. Gibran’s departure, “Prabowo is attempting to demonstrate that he has stepped out from Jokowi’s shadow,” he added, referring to the former leader by his nickname.

Initially a political outsider, Mr. Joko came to be seen as a transformative leader of Indonesia. He helped rehabilitate the image of Mr. Prabowo, a former special forces commander who was discharged from the military for his involvement in the abduction and torture of pro-democracy activists under the dictator Suharto. To many, Mr. Prabowo was a reminder of the country’s authoritarian past. But Mr. Joko endorsed Mr. Prabowo as his successor, with Mr. Gibran running on the same ticket.

The alliance between Mr. Joko and Mr. Prabowo was cast as one of political continuity. Many voters had seen Mr. Gibran’s vice presidency as a way for Mr. Joko to continue his political influence.

“From the beginning, Gibran was brought on board solely to serve as a ‘vote-getter,’ rather than as an integral part of a unified leadership team intended to strengthen the performance of the Prabowo administration,” said Titi Anggraini, a lecturer of constitutional law at University of Indonesia.

#244. FAA lifts nationwide ground stop that blocked takeoffs for hours

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A communications problem shut down takeoffs across the US on Friday afternoon before it was lifted more than four hours later.

A communications problem shut down takeoffs across the US on Friday afternoon before it was lifted more than four hours later.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Takeoffs from airports throughout the US largely stopped on Friday after the FAA issued a national ground stop at 3:44PM ET “due to extraordinary circumstances and for reasons of safety.”

More than four hours after the stop was issued, officials announced it was lifted at 8:15PM ET and that flights will resume. It’s unclear how many flights will be able to take off and when, as they now face problems of overloaded airports and flight crews that have run out of time to operate the planes, meaning many people will be stuck where they are for the time being and more flights will be delayed or canceled.

In a White House press conference announcing that the stop had been lifted, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy blamed an aging air traffic control system that he is pursuing funds to replace, saying that the TRACONs (Terminal Radar Approach Control) in the air traffic control centers “started to have inconsistency in the data from the radars and the communications from airplanes.”

He said that the devices that had the issue were analog telecom devices, where the switches / routers “got overloaded with data” and started to show inconsistency.

An earlier report from CBS News cited multiple sources who said that an update to the air traffic control system’s private communications network, operated by L3Harris, caused a system reboot and the subsequent outage. L3Harris has not yet responded to a request for comment from The Verge, but during the press conference, Duffy and Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin confirmed that “patches” to the existing system sparked the issue.

In response to a reporter’s question about the patches and how they contributed to the outage, Duffy said, “By doing nothing, you inject way more risk into the system… I didn’t anticipate that we would have an outage like this.”

The flight tracking site FlightAware lists 9,124 US flights as delayed and 5,901 flights canceled as of 8:39PM ET.

According to CNN, the last nationwide ground stop in 2023 lasted for about 90 minutes after the NOTAM (Notice to Air Missions) system for pilots went down, and the last one prior to that was after the attacks on September 11th, 2001.

Speaking to reporters outside the White House on Friday afternoon, Mullin told reporters that there is currently no reason to suspect a cyberattack or other national security threat, or any connection to the recent Microsoft Outlook outage, but that at the time no cause had been ruled out for the “telecommunications outage.”

When asked about any possible connection to the cloud-based AI Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes and Trajectories (SMART) system that was activated nationwide on November 10th, he said they were looking into that. Air Space Intelligence (ASI), the company that designed the system and was awarded a 12-year $875 million contract in June to deploy it, denied any connection, tweeting “The current ground stop is not based on or a result of the SMART system, any product of ASI or our integration with FAA and ATC.”

Jon Ostrower of The Air Current reports that the outage began with the Chicago air traffic control center, followed by centers in Boston, Washington, New York, and Minneapolis, and as the FAA issued a ground stop for aircraft flying east, more facilities began reporting problems. He said the problems disrupted both the radar and radio frequencies used by air traffic controllers and pilots.

Updated, Friday, November 13th: Added that the stop has been lifted, and quotes from the press conference.

#241. Jensen Huang and the decline of the visionary showman founder CEO

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Steve Jobs of GPUs has been shown the door.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

On January 6th, 2025, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang got his rockstar moment at last. He strode triumphantly onstage, hand raised in the air, to the claps and cheers of a 14,000-person arena absolutely packed with influencers and fans, their faces awash in blue glow, as he made his entrance in a black leather jacket that shimmered in the stadium’s lights.

Few are cheering for Jensen Huang now. He has left Nvidia after seemingly enabling two chip smuggling scandals and allegedly having three extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy at the same time he was mingling with President Donald Trump.

The world is watching to see if this scandal might disrupt the entire US AI industry and perhaps destabilize the stock market along with it. That’s because Huang wasn’t merely running the first $5 trillion company, he was the visionary leader in control of the must-have GPUs powering the worldwide AI boom, part of a company that represents around 6 percent of the S&P 500 stock market index all by itself. And he didn’t plan for succession if he ever stepped down.

The 63-year-old once said he preferred to die on the job; he once suggested he’d work until he was 90 or 100, then carry on leading the company as a robot.

It’s not easy to replace Huang, partly because he had no deputies. Around 60 employees reported directly to the CEO. “He built the company around himself, and everything that happened there flowed through him,” says IDC analyst and former computing journalist Tom Mainelli.

“Jensen is Nvidia, and Nvidia is Jensen,” Jon Peddie, a computer industry analyst who’s been tracking Nvidia since its inception, tells me.

But Nvidia isn’t Jensen anymore. He has left the company he cofounded 33 years ago. It’s the end of an era, and not just for Nvidia — because Jensen Huang was Big Tech’s last true example of a visionary showman founder CEO.

“He was both the visionary and the tactical leader, the closest thing tech has had to Steve Jobs,” Moor Insights & Strategy chief analyst Pat Moorhead tells me.

Elon Musk didn’t exactly found Tesla and has quite the track record of broken promises, and Meta’s Mark Zuckerberg has proven to be neither visionary nor showman so far. Google’s Larry Page and Sergey Brin stepped away in 2019 as their company dealt with growing employee turmoil, leaving the company in the hands of Sundar Pichai, and both Microsoft and Apple have each passed the torch twice since the days of Bill Gates and Steve Jobs.

Some say Apple’s Steve Jobs broke the CEO mold, but he created a mold of his own. Huang was one of countless tech leaders who followed his example by introducing their products in keynote speeches, adopting a single recognizable piece of clothing like Jobs’ trademark black turtleneck, and even occasionally teasing “one more thing” for the surprise at the end of their presentations. In the newsroom, we’d sometimes joke about the Jobs wannabes; many were stiff suits who didn’t have the credibility to pull it off.

I thought Huang might be a Jobs wannabe, too, the first time I saw that he’d adopted a black jacket as his uniform. (It was not always a leather jacket; an earlier version was a Nvidia-branded softshell.) Huang later insisted on wearing a jacket even when the temperature was hot — more than once, I saw him sweat profusely rather than take it off. Unlike Jobs, he also held quite a few keynotes when he didn’t have much news to share; he liked to repeat the company’s previous accomplishments as if they were brand-new.

But even if Huang didn’t have the same gravitas as Jobs, he did have exciting tech up his sleeves and loved to demo it onstage. He knew how to get people excited about the future of visual computing with eye-opening gaming graphics, physics and lighting demos, and eventually image recognition, AI weather simulations, digital copies of real-world factories, and the robots that can virtually train in them.

His Nvidia tried to educate people about the how and why of GPUs, too; I still remember in 2008 when Nvidia invited the Mythbusters to demo the difference between serial processing and parallel processing by firing a 1,100-nozzle paintball gun to “paint” the Mona Lisa in one shot.

What I didn’t know back then: Huang was a visionary founder. He took Nvidia to fame and fortune through a series of tremendous bets.

In 1996, three years after he cofounded the company at a Denny’s diner, Nvidia nearly went out of business while working on a failed graphics chip for the Sega Dreamcast game console. But even though he didn’t deliver that chip, Huang personally convinced Sega to invest $5 million afterward, The Wall Street Journal reported in 2024.

Even without a named successor, analysts don’t think Nvidia is in big trouble yet.

“This is a stock shock, not a business shock, for the next year. The roadmap and manufacturing are locked in through 2027,” says Pat Moorhead, Moor Insights & Strategy. “The question is whether Nvidia keeps inventing at Jensen’s pace once those plans run out. The bigger risk than any rival chip is customers hedging during a vacuum. That is why the next CEO needs technical credibility, an operating model that doesn’t depend on one person, and a plan to protect CUDA.”

“Founder visionary, culture setter, leader, AI industry spokesperson etc., is the real loss. We don’t think anything changes strategically,” says Creative Strategies analyst Ben Bajarin.” The product roadmap is set and remains strong, and the next several generations are already in design, in tape-out or on order. NVIDIA is still the company best placed to ship its roadmap at scale, because it has spent years locking up the supply chain behind it, from leading-edge wafers and CoWoS-class packaging to HBM and the rack-level partners. That supply position does not walk out the door with one person.”

“He was the consummate showman, salesman, and visionary for NVIDIA, so he will be hard to replace,” says IDC analyst Tom Mainelli. “But the company’s technology road map is sound, and the team behind him is still there. Choosing the right person to replace him will be key, and there will undoubtedly be bumps along the way. But the company will survive.”

“Jensen built an exceptionally talented organization with a deep engineering bench, a mature, heavily-resourced software ecosystem, and strong customer relationships,” says Dave Altavilla, HotTech Vision and Analysis. “His departure undoubtedly leaves a leadership and strategic vision gap, but the technology, talent, and execution capabilities he helped establish aren’t going away. The real question is whether his successor can sustain that same long-term vision and willingness to invest ahead of the market.”

“No one in the company really wants to step into his shoes, so an outsider is going to have to be found,” says Jon Peddie of Jon Peddie Research. “Whoever that is will not be Jensen, won’t have the same ambitions, the same contacts and relationships, the same vision, or the same courage to make big, long-term bets. We’ve seen this in Apple, AMD, Intel, and other companies. So the stock will probably get battered around a bit until investors see some stability and fewer ‘what if’ questions.”

He then turned around and bet the company again on the Riva 128 PC graphics accelerator, an effort so ambitious he had to lay off over half of Nvidia’s roughly 100 staffers, skip the prototyping stage, and rush it straight to production, and he still only had one month of payroll left by the time it shipped, The New Yorker wrote in 2023. Thankfully, it was a success, selling a million units in just four months and paving the way for both the GeForce GPU and a long-term relationship with chip fabricator TSMC.

Roughly eight years later, Huang decided it wasn’t enough to be the leader in PC gaming graphics alone. He bet the company’s entire gross profit, he told podcaster Lex Fridman, on shipping a new feature inside those graphics cards that no one had asked for: CUDA. It was the ability to use a GPU for other kinds of computing tasks beyond graphics, and Huang said it increased the cost of every GeForce graphics card by 50 percent.

“Originally there was no market or market demand for it. It was almost as if they built it because they could,” says Peddie. “Jensen had the vision for what it could be and the courage to pursue it in spite of the opinions of others.”

“NVIDIA was effectively investing in a computing platform that extended far beyond graphics, with no guarantee of a near-term return,” says HotTech analyst Dave Altavilla.

“Huang saw that a GPU was really a parallel computer that happened to render graphics. CUDA turned that insight into a platform, and it’s why NVIDIA was ready when AI arrived,” Mainelli tells me.

It was a bad time for graphics cards to suddenly cost more. When the Great Recession hit in 2007–2009, and Nvidia laptop graphics chips also started failing due to design defects, the company lost 80 percent of its market value.

But Huang believed in CUDA enough to invest even more. “He literally handed out high-end NVIDIA GPUs to researchers at industry conferences, while the company seeded universities like Stanford with hardware to encourage development on its platform,” says Altavilla. And by 2012, Nvidia’s potential for AI had started to emerge.

That’s when University of Toronto researchers used Nvidia GPUs to train a prize-winning image-recognition neural network called AlexNet, which is widely credited for sparking the AI revolution.

Not all of Huang’s visions panned out the way he publicly expected. That AlexNet demo directly led to Huang’s bets on chips for self-driving cars, he said, but he didn’t exactly predict AI — CUDA was instead thought of as better and more affordable supercomputing for researchers and scientists. In 2013, I also wrote about how his grand plan for the future of the company relied heavily on putting Nvidia chips in your phone, which didn’t pan out.

But that bet on ARM-based phone chips did put Nvidia inside every Nintendo Switch and may have helped make future “AI superchips” possible — the latest Nvidia AI server chips have ARM CPU cores. And in 2022, the leaders of the AI revolution launched the most groundbreaking AI tech yet on Nvidia hardware: ChatGPT. Six years earlier, Huang hand-delivered Nvidia’s first purpose-built AI workstation to OpenAI, and by 2022 Microsoft had strung together tens of thousands of Nvidia A100 GPUs to handle the load.

“Every time competitors got close, he moved the fight up a level: the chip, then the server, then networking with the $6.9 billion Mellanox deal, then the full rack, all tied together by CUDA,” says Moorhead. “The speed came from how he ran the company,” he adds. “Decisions that took other companies four weeks took Nvidia four days.”

“Decisions that took other companies four weeks took Nvidia four days.”

As Nvidia’s fortunes and profit ballooned — thousands of dollars in pure profit every single second! — and as it became clearer that AI would reshape the world in one way or another, something strange happened. Huang became a celebrity. In summer 2024, The Wall Street Journal and social media chronicled that he received a hero’s welcome in his birthplace of Taiwan. He stopped over and over to sign autographs, including one on a woman’s chest.

In person, at presentations, and in the company’s investor Q&As, I saw the tone shift. People began treating the leather-jacketed man like an actual rockstar instead of a wannabe rockstar, asking for photos and autographs and complimenting him rather than asking tough questions.

It felt like he’d attained Steve Jobs’ infamous “reality distortion field” at last — until he popped that bubble himself with his infidelity.

But one fewer reality distortion field in the world isn’t necessarily a bad thing, and as Apple has shown, a company doesn’t go bust just because its charismatic founder is gone. Tim Cook made Apple the most profitable company in the world. The question now is: who for Nvidia?

“He was the king,” says Peddie. “Make no mistake – it was the Jensen Huang company. He called all the shots, took all the glory, and shouldered all the blame.”

Hi, friends! Welcome to Installer No. 152, your guide to the best and Verge-iest stuff in the world. (If you’re new here, welcome, thank you for taking time away from Zelda to be here, and also you can read all the old editions at the Installer homepage.)

This week, I’ve been reading about school apps and AI benches and sports gambling and Megaspeed, watching the surprisingly emotional Dane Cook documentary, learning way more than I ever expected about dial-up sounds, digging out my old Xbox One since it’s apparently my best shot at playing GTA VI, seeing what I can get done with Claude Duet, and trying a bunch of third-party iPhone keyboards in the hope of voice-typing my way through everything forever.

I also have for you a great new space history performance, new apps for writers and podcast listeners, a fun podcast about Wikipedia, and much more. Let’s dig in.

A lot of you out there in the Installerverse have become iPhone Duo users over the last few weeks, and I’ve heard a lot about how you’re all using Apple’s new foldable phone. (I’d say the sentiment in my inbox is roughly 80 percent positive, 5 percent negative, 15 percent deeply confused about interface choices.) I’ve also gotten some questions about which Duo apps are great, and how best to set yours up.

Alas, I do not have a Duo. So I turned to The Verge’s Allison Johnson, who coauthored our review of the device and has used it as aggressively as anyone I know. I asked her to share how she’s wielding the Duo, and she sent over a truly unhinged color scheme and otherwise a bunch of very good ideas. Her’s her homescreen, plus some info on the apps she uses and why:

The wallpaper: It’s one of those color gradient options you can pick in iOS. I was trying to go for a minimalist thing.

The apps: NYT Games, Kindle, Headspace, Spotify, Google Maps, Safari, Messages, Mail.

So I tried the whole aspirational “dress for the homescreen you want” thing for a few days, with just a few apps like Headspace and NYT Games, and it was a miserable failure. It just made me mad every time I had to search for Messages. I added a row of essential apps to the bottom of the screen and a big calendar widget, and things have been much better.

There’s a big widget / shortcut to TypeDraw, which is a fantastic app on the Duo that makes the whole phone behave like a physical notebook. It’s great with the Apple Pencil. I also realized I need weather close by, so I added a giant widget to the left panel of the homescreen.

I also have a shortcut for the built-in lo-fi playlists on iOS, which is handy if I need to pop in earbuds and play something instrumental so I can focus real quick. Highly recommended to anyone who hasn’t discovered these yet and also gets overstimulated! They’re already on your phone!

I did nuke the dock entirely in my minimalist experiment and I’m actually fine with it. Maybe I’ll never go back to the dock again.

I also asked Allison to share a few things she’s into right now. Here’s what she sent back:

Here’s what the Installer community is into this week. I want to know what you’re into right now as well! Email installer@theverge.com or message me on Signal — @davidpierce.11 — with your recommendations for anything and everything, and we’ll feature some of our favorites here every week.

“Search, a browser developed by a small Parisian design studio of all things. I’d say they have created the classy European cousin of Arc, based on Webkit so it’s super light & responsive on Mac while also having the flexibility of Chrome extensions.” — Elliot

“Trying out Monarch to potentially break my years-long Quicken habit. So far I’m about 90% sure I’m switching.” — Bruce

“Dungeon Crawler Carl. I recently started listening to the audiobook. It has consumed my life. I listen to it on my drive to work, I take breaks at work to listen to it, I play it throughout my day, and eventually fall asleep listening to it. It’s almost 200 hours of content and Jeff Hays does an amazing job voicing all the characters. I’m currently on book seven of eight, and not looking forward to the withdrawals as we wait for book 9. Seriously, check it out.” — William

“Brenpoly on YouTube has open-sourced his “I built an Apple Vision Pro for $5” project. It’s a great watch even if you don’t dabble in 3D printing. It also shows how much potential there is for affordable VR with just the phones in our pockets and some very clever people and communities.” — Owen

“For anyone with an iPhone Duo. On the outer display, Safari keeps a column down the side for its buttons, and web pages stop short of it. I’m a Duo owner, and am now using a great Safari extension called Widen that runs pages underneath that column: ~25% more page, with text kept clear of the buttons.” — Marcus

“I took my kid to see Wildwood, the new Laika movie, this past weekend and left the theater with my jaw on the floor and dragging a few feet behind me. It is extraordinary. For me, put it up there with the all time Studio Ghibli greats. (I know, I know, but I’m serious.) In a way that no Marvel movie special effects have done, I sat at multiple times in the movie thinking, ‘I just can’t believe humans made this.’” — Stephen

“Queen’s Domain, a wonderful King’s Field-like game, was released this past weekend and it conjures up all those familiar FromSoftware feelings of combat and exploration! Highly recommended, it’s quite the experience.” — Kyle

“I finished Jet Lag: The Game’s Japanorama, and it is the absolute best tourism video series for Japan without trying at all to be one. It is part scavenger hunt, part race across Japan, and shows off the wonderful scenery and the beautiful public transit of the country. I learnt that Japan loves making lists (OCD folks rejoice!) and has lists of Three Views, Three Great Gardens, 100 Best Sunsets, 100 Remarkable Waters and many more.” — Utkarsh

I’m working on a story about the new era of hardware hacking, in which people are suddenly discovering they can vibe-code their way to a better TV, the e-reader of their dreams, and a million other things besides. In almost all my conversations, one thing keeps coming up: the M5Stack developer kits. This company will send you an ESP32-based device you can hack to bits, whether you’re looking for something with an E Ink display or something with a round touchscreen. I’m about to spend an alarming amount of money buying these things, and an equally alarming amount of time making Claude Code do weird stuff with them. I’m already stealing ideas from the fabulous M5Stack subreddit. If you get up to something fun while hardware hacking this weekend, tell me all about it.

#237. In China’s Spy Operations, Romance Can Be a Long Game

Jensen Huang’s affair with a woman U.S. officials say is a Chinese spy has drawn attention to the role of intimate relationships in espionage.

At the height of the Cold War, a top official in China’s intelligence agency said that his country would never use seduction to entrap a person.

Such tactics might be for the C.I.A. or the K.G.B., the official named Kong Yuan, of the Central Investigation Department, told a group of agents in training in April 1960, but China was guided by principles. He looked at the few female comrades in the audience. “Rest assured, we will not be asking you to go run beauty traps.”

Despite that pledge, captured in a 2010 memoir by a former Chinese diplomat, China’s intelligence services have for decades used romantic entanglements to compromise or extract information from prominent people.

American officials say that China’s Ministry of State Security, which runs intelligence operations today, recruited a Chinese businesswoman to collect intelligence on Jensen Huang. Mr. Huang, who resigned on Thursday as the chief executive of Nvidia, has been one of the most powerful figures in the artificial intelligence boom.

It was not clear what role the woman, Alice Huang, or Huang Le, might have had with the Chinese spy agency. Ms. Huang was a top executive of a Singapore company that operates data centers in Southeast Asia using Nvidia chips and that rents their computing power to Chinese firms.

Mr. Huang has acknowledged that he had an affair with Ms. Huang. President Trump has said that Mr. Huang told him that he was not aware that the woman was a spy. China has rejected the espionage allegations against Ms. Huang, calling them smears against the country. U.S. officials have not provided evidence for their claims. Ms. Huang has not responded to requests for comment.
