#156. SpaceX not being honest about ‘political’ reasons for slowing Starshield, says Zelenskyy

Throttling of Elon Musk’s military-grade satellite system affecting whole frontline, says Ukraine’s president

Volodymyr Zelenskyy has accused SpaceX of not telling the truth over restrictions to Ukraine’s Starshield satellite system, saying the decision to impose speed limits appears to be “political” rather than “technical”.

Speaking on Friday, Ukraine’s president said the restrictions affecting Ukrainian drones had been in place for five days. They could no longer fly at speeds of more than 161km/h (100mph), he said, with connections at higher speeds lost.

As a result, incoming jet-powered Russian Shahed drones could no longer be intercepted. Ukraine’s own mid-range strikes against military targets in Russian-occupied territory were also severely affected, he said.

Speaking to the Guardian and other journalists at the presidential palace in Kyiv, Zelenskyy said he had asked his military leadership to raise the issue with Elon Musk’s company SpaceX, as well as with the Pentagon and the US state department.

There was “no clear explanation” as to why Ukraine’s operations were being throttled. He said SpaceX had told Kyiv that there were “no restrictions”. The claim, Zelenskyy suggested, was untrue with problems detected across the “entire frontline”.

“There have been discussions with the company at the institutional level. The ministry of defence spoke with SpaceX. They replied: ‘We are not imposing any restrictions.’ They say it is some kind of technical issue. We do not believe that is the case,” Zelenskyy said.

He added: “The question is not whether we believe them or not. There may be some further checks. But every day, we test this in different directions. Every day, we get the same confirmation. So these are not isolated cases; they are widespread.”

Ukraine suspects the slowdown is a deliberate US tactic, designed to put pressure on Zelenskyy to make concessions to Russia’s president, Vladimir Putin. It follows a similar issue last month, and coincided with US-brokered talks last week in Abu Dhabi featuring Ukrainian and Russian representatives.

The Kremlin insists Ukraine give up the entire eastern Donbas region.

But Zelenskyy categorically ruled out giving away further land to Russian “terrorists”. He also revealed that US negotiators, Donald Trump’s son-in-law Jared Kushner, and real estate developer Steve Witkoff, had asked if Ukraine was willing to give up the Donbas in exchange for membership of Nato, or Nato-like security guarantees.

Details of the proposal were vague, Zelenskyy said. They did not appear to involve US peacekeeping troops on the ground, he added. He said the war could only end with a ceasefire agreement, alongside meaningful security guarantees, underwritten by western partners.

Zelenskyy also dismissed an offer by Putin for a “humanitarian mission” in the eastern city of Kostiantynivka as a ploy. Russia has called for a five-day ceasefire, modelled on a truce last month in the Russian-occupied town of Oleshky, from where civilians were evacuated.

“It’s an absolutely primitive move. Putin simply wants us to leave Kostiantynivka so that they can move in and occupy the entire city that way,” Zelenskyy said. He said civilians had already fled the area as fierce street-by-street fighting continued, with huge Russian losses.

Zelenskyy said he had proposed repeating the Oleshky truce in nearby Russian-occupied villages and towns in south Ukraine, along the Dnipro River. Putin rejected this. His true goal was to swallow up Kostiantynivka and the Ukrainian garrison cities of Sloviansk and Kramatorsk, Zelenskyy said.

He also called Russian attempts to discuss Europe’s security architecture – a topic raised last week by the Trump administration – as “concerning”. The Kremlin has called for Nato to return to its pre-1997 configuration, before eastern European countries including the Baltic states joined the alliance.

Putin first made this demand in 2021, in the runup to his big Ukraine invasion. Its revival after nearly five years of full-scale war, Zelenskyy said, was an alarming signal, indicative of imperial thinking. “Putin’s desire to change borders in Europe is a fact,” Zelenskyy said, stressing: “We cannot turn everything upside down.”

Ukraine’s president said his recent conversations with European leaders who met in Dublin had brought tangible results. They included pledges from Denmark to supply ammunition for F-16 jets – used to shoot down Russian drones – as well as energy support packages for Kyiv and other cities.

Russia was “betting everything” on its winter campaign, he added. In recent months Moscow has escalated its attacks on Ukrainian cities, in an attempt to make civilians capitulate. The US Congress could pressure Putin to de-escalate by passing laws on sanctions and giving Ukraine weapons, Zelenskyy said.

#152. ‘Enough is enough’: rising number of deadly ebike battery fires spark fear in New York City

City has failed to build infrastructure that could reduce threat, and saw 400 fires, 83 injuries and 12 deaths this year

Fires from lithium-ion batteries, which power ebikes and scooters, have become regular occurrences in New York, causing deaths and fear among residents, but the city has failed to build infrastructure that could reduce the threat.

New York has seen a dramatic uptick this year in fires from the batteries. In 2025, there were 292 fires, 51 injuries and one death, according to the New York fire department (FDNY). This year, there have already been more than 400 fires, 83 injuries and 12 deaths.

In late October, four members of a family, including an 11-year-old boy, died in a fire caused by a battery in an ebike that was in the pathway to an exit of an 11th floor apartment in a public housing building in the Bronx.

Then recently in a fire at a home in Brooklyn, six people died and nine were injured, including three firefighters and two police officers. Investigators discovered lithium-ion batteries at the scene but have not determined whether they caused the fire, the city council speaker, Julie Menin, said in a statement.

“The presence of lithium-ion batteries at the scene is a serious concern, and more must be done to encourage and mandate safe storage practices. Enough is enough,” Menin stated.

London and Toronto have also recently seen a dramatic increase in lithium-ion battery fires, which are often caused by overcharging and defective or damaged batteries.

Some of that trend is simply because ebikes, often used by food delivery workers and commuters, have become more popular.

But in New York, the city has not executed its plan to build outdoor charging stations which would prevent people from plugging in the batteries in their apartments, where an overheated or defective battery poses a greater risk, experts say.

“There is a ton of red tape around this sort of new technology, and while it’s well intended to not let something new roll out too quickly, I think in this case, it is time to do something quickly,” said Laura Kavanagh, former commissioner of the FDNY.

In 2025, there were 2.2m ebikes imported into the United States, according to Light Electric Vehicle Association, double the number from just three years earlier.

In response to the increased usage and related increase in fires, the New York City department of transportation conducted a 2024 pilot in which it hired companies to offer battery-swapping cabinets where delivery workers could exchange exhausted batteries for charged ones.

The riders did more than 12,000 swaps over six months, and among participants, there was a 35% reduction in charging batteries at home, according to the city.

Following its success, the city announced that it would install 25 charging sites but not until 2027. The city first needed to “gather feedback on site selection, design and implementation”.

In April, New York mayor Zohran Mamdani asked residents to provide feedback on where they would like the 25 cabinets. The city planned to make them available in 2028, the announcement stated.

In 2023, the city also announced that it received a $25m federal grant to build 173 electric-micromobility charging and storage stations at 53 public housing developments.

“This means that residents will no longer need to charge their ebikes in their apartments, what we find to be extremely dangerous,” Mayor Eric Adams said at the time.

At the time, the deputy mayor, Meera Joshi, said residents would start “seeing evidence” of the stations in early 2024.

Even though the Biden administration allocated the $25m for the infrastructure, the federal government has not actually provided the funds, according to the New York department of transportation.

Asked why the process has moved slowly, a transportation department spokesperson stated in an email: “The city’s strategy to reduce the risk e-bike battery fires focuses on getting unsafe devices off our streets and providing safe and convenient e-bike charging options” and the “outdoor battery swapping cabinets will make charging safer and more accessible for the thousands of delivery workers who rely on these devices to serve New Yorkers. With the public feedback we gathered this summer, we’re finalizing locations for our public e-bike charging program before advancing to the design phase and remain on pace to launch the service in 2028”.

Meanwhile, Popwheels, one of the companies in the successful pilot, has installed about 45 cabinets around the city, according to CEO Baruch Herzfeld.

He wants to install hundreds more around the city but has been stymied by a permitting process that he describes as “ludicrous”. He said it often takes six months for the city to approve one cabinet.

“If I want to put a cabinet that they approve in a parking lot, I have to use an architect to do a drawing, and then they have to come and do an inspection of a drawing of an ebike battery cabinet in the parking lot,” Herzfeld said. “This permitting process is why kids are dying.”

The city also often issues tickets to the owners of properties where Herzfeld has installed the cabinets, he said, for minor infractions like a fire extinguisher not having the proper tags or trash at the site.

Amanda Farinacci, the FDNY deputy commissioner public information, stated in an email that the tickets were not for “minor infractions but instead significant concerns”.

“The FDNY has been very serious partners with the micro-mobility/charging cabinet industry, being very accessible and collaborative to achieve solutions,” Farinacci stated. “We are a public safety agency and our mission is public safety. To conflate our regulatory responsibilities with causing people to charge their batteries indoors is entirely misplaced and inaccurate.”

Kavanagh, who spent a decade at FDNY before leaving in 2024, argued that the department is focusing on “too narrow of a concern”.

“I respect whatever concerns they have about the cabinet as real, but that is asking the wrong question,” said Kavanagh, who now works as a visiting urbanist at New York University. If “a battery failed in that cabinet outside, it is almost certain that would do no more than property damage. You are comparing that to” 10 New Yorkers dying in fires in the last three weeks.

“This is just appreciably worse, and so I think we need to frame it that way,” she added.

The latest fire may spur the city to expedite its approval process. On Friday, Menin announced that the city council would hold an emergency meeting on 20 November to conduct oversight to ensure the city is properly enforcing existing laws and that the public is educated on best fire safety practices and lithium-ion battery storage.

Ebike fires “are something that can cause people to lose their lives as well as their livelihoods”, Mamdani said on MS Now after the Brooklyn fire. “And so, we are really looking at every avenue we have to keep them safe.”

#151. Microsoft’s Global Outage and Fixes: What We Know

The company described “encouraging” progress on efforts to overcome a second day of disruptions to its email, messaging and other services.

Microsoft is reporting widespread improvements to its services after a global outage took them offline on Thursday, disrupting communications and work at schools, government agencies and offices around the world. The company, headquartered in Redmond, Wash., said on Friday that it was seeing “encouraging recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure.”

“We remain focused on addressing residual impact and ensuring reliable service restoration,” the company added.

Here’s what to know as of Friday afternoon.

Outages started disrupting Microsoft tools and apps around noon Eastern time on Thursday and quickly spread to more than 70 countries and territories, affecting millions of users, according to Downdetector, an outage-tracking site.

Some of Microsoft’s most popular products, including the email manager Outlook, the workplace messaging and video calling app Teams and the artificial intelligence assistant Copilot, stopped working.

Microsoft said in a statement on Friday that “for now, we can confirm that there is no indication this is the result of malicious or cyber activity.” It added that the problem was “a technical issue not related to cyber or A.I.” agents.

The company said it had no indication that a Federal Aviation Administration ground stop issued on late Friday afternoon and affecting air travel nationwide was related to the outage.

#150. Health is wealth? Polymarket and Kalshi now host bets on measles and other US health crises

Experts warn of risks of platforms hosting bets tied to US public health – including vaccine mandates and drug trials

The sign was pasted on a construction fence next to an elementary school in the Brooklyn borough of New York City. “Vaccines,” it proclaimed in bold capital letters against a blue star-spangled background – appearing, at first glance, to be like any other public health message on the importance of getting vaccinated.

But at the bottom of the sign, in smaller letters, it read: “Will any state end vaccine markets?” It was accompanied by the logo for Polymarket, an online prediction market where users can log in to wager on the outcomes of nearly any event – including, now, public health.

Polymarket joins Kalshi, another online prediction market, which began offering health-related predictions over the summer. Users can wager on when the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) will approve a skin-cancer vaccine, or whether the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) will change its vaccine recommendations. They can also bet on drug trial outcomes and approval; they can bet on how many cases of measles the US might see in 2026 and whether it will lose its measles-elimination status this year.

“I am not surprised,” said Matthew Eisenberg, professor and chair of health policy and management at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health. “Kalshi and Polymarket will list contracts on anything with a checkable outcome.”

Despite being functionally “identical” to regulated gambling, prediction markets are largely unregulated, said Lia Nower, a professor and the director of the Center for Gambling Studies at Rutgers University.

“Gambling right now is like the wild, wild west,” she added. “There’s no federal regulatory body that’s really prescribing any limits on any of this, or just minimum guidelines for states to follow – so anything’s possible.”

Prediction markets position themselves as financial derivatives, rather than traditional bets, in a regulatory loophole, Nower said – which means they operate under federal commodities laws, with oversight from the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission, and are not regulated by states as gambling operations. Several states have filed lawsuits against the companies alleging that they function as gambling sites.

Advocates, officials and lawmakers could place bets on ending vaccine mandates at the state level, for instance, and then introduce new laws or policies to do so; there don’t appear to be any laws against that, Nower said. There are bets on when certain officials will leave health agencies, which could be affected by resignation or termination decisions.

Predictions could also affect how drugs are tested and regulated, critics say. Trial participants could purposefully change their behavior to affect outcomes; drug companies could change the timing of trials.

Predictions on FDA decisions and trial endpoints “create a payoff for anyone with nonpublic information”, like agency reviewers, trial investigators and legislative staff, Eisenberg said. Prediction markets may create their own safeguards, like employment checks at sign-up, “but the risk isn’t necessarily that a reviewer takes a bribe – it’s that the market now rewards leaks”, he said.

Polymarket did not respond to the Guardian’s questions about the potential consequences for public health or medicine. Kalshi did not respond on the record to the same questions, but spokesperson Dani Lever argued that the predictions on drug trials and approvals would improve transparency.

“Kalshi’s clinical trial markets are a pilot program aimed at a structural problem in drug development: the people who best know whether a trial will succeed are scattered, and their insight rarely reaches the public,” Lever said in a statement. “Public markets avoid those barriers.”

Problem gambling is itself a rapidly growing public health concern.

Gambling is a “big public health issue” that disproportionately affects people who are younger, Black or Hispanic and lower-income, Nower said. She co-authored a new report showing that more than half of young people, between the ages of 13 and 27, gambled in the past year – and among the young gamblers, 39.5% had some degree of gambling-related risk and 10.3% were high risk for problem gambling.

Eisenberg’s research on online sports betting found that legalization is followed by measurable increases in treatment for depression and anxiety among young men. There have been some discussions after such findings to create state-level consumer protections – like deposit limits, self-exclusion, rules about advertising and funding for treatment paid out of state betting taxes, he said. “But the prediction markets sit outside all of that.” Very little of the revenue flows to states through taxes to support such treatment.

“In effect, these are gambling products that have exited the only regulatory system designed to limit gambling harm,” Eisenberg said.

Prediction markets are “really something that the government needs to clamp down on”, Nower said. Yet “the federal government still has no federal regulatory body,” she added. There are no research centers at the National Institutes of Health focused on gambling, as there are for alcohol and drug abuse.

“It’s just unaddressed at the federal level,” Nower said.

States like New Jersey are moving to restrict the markets – and concerns include consumer protection and the potential for insider trading, Nower said.

“People with inside information or control over an outcome could potentially profit from what they call ‘event contracts’,” another word for the predictions, she said.

This is the future, Eisenberg said: “Gambling on everything, everywhere, with fewer and fewer consumer protections.”

Yet he pointed out that public-health propositions on the markets were small, and he cautioned against seeing them as any sign of public sentiment. “Prices that small can be moved by a single trader and shouldn’t be read as public opinion,” Eisenberg said. “A few-hundred-dollar contract on the childhood vaccine schedule is not a forecast of anything.”

Within a week, the Polymarket sign in Brooklyn was vandalized. The bottom, with the Polymarket logo and the text on placing a prediction, was ripped away.

#148. Noel Fielding ‘horrified and deeply disturbed’ by Russell Brand’s crimes

Comedian regrets ‘inappropriate’ jokes he made with Brand during work pair did together in the 2000s

The Great British Bake Off presenter Noel Fielding has said he was “horrified and deeply disturbed to learn of Russell Brand’s crimes” and has had no contact with his fellow comedian “for many years”.

In the early 2000s, Fielding often worked with Brand, who was sentenced to more than 10 years in prison on Monday for raping one woman and sexually assaulting three others.

In a statement issued to the Sun, Fielding said: “I was horrified and deeply disturbed to learn of Russell Brand’s crimes, which I was completely unaware of until they became public knowledge. My thoughts are with the women whose courage brought him to justice.

“I appeared on TV, radio and live charity shows with him, and whilst we played it up for the cameras and in the media, I considered it a professional relationship, rather than a personal one. I have had no contact with him for many years.

“Looking back at some of the jokes I made alongside him, I was appalled. They were horrible and inappropriate, and I very much regret them.”

Footage has recently resurfaced of Fielding and Brand joking about rape with the TV presenter Jonathan Ross on an episode of the Big Fat Quiz of the Year in 2007. Brand also joked with Fielding about kissing and holding an underage “grasshopper” on an episode of The Russell Brand Show which aired in October 2007.

Fielding, a frequent guest and co-host on the BBC Radio 2 show, said: “How do you even know if it’s a child or an adult? You don’t even care, do you?”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Brand, who was 32 at the time, replied: “It’s a mistake I’ve made. I’ve had a lot of problems in the past, because I thought a grasshopper was above age. Before I know it, I’m in it, right, I’m kissing it, I’m holding it. Then it turns around and looks at me with onyx eyes and says: ‘I’m 12.’”

The pair had met at the NME Awards in the early 2000s and often performed at charity gigs as a comedy duo known as The Goth Detectives.

Fielding has never been accused of misconduct and there is no suggestion he knew about Brand’s behaviour towards women. Since his conviction, Brand was accused of sexual misconduct and predatory behaviour by a further six women in a new investigation by the Times and the Sunday Times.

Information and support for anyone affected by rape or sexual abuse issues is available from the following organisations. In the UK, Rape Crisis offers support on 0808 500 2222 in England and Wales, 0808 801 0302 in Scotland, or 0800 0246 991 in Northern Ireland. In the US, Rainn offers support on 800-656-4673. In Australia, support is available at 1800Respect (1800 737 732). Other international helplines can be found at ibiblio.org/rcip/internl.html

#146. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.
